Chapter 1

CHAPTER 1

Cement dust settled in the boy’s hair, a fine grey snow. He watched the last wall of his house come down, the future a flat, empty lot. His father’s watch, heavy in his pocket, ticked against his thigh. Yesterday was a pile of rubble. Tomorrow was just more dust.



The bell rang.

It was a thin, tinny sound, the bell of someone who has nothing left to announce. Leo didn’t move. He watched the river. The bell rang again, two short, insistent jabs. He placed the cup on the sill, the porcelain clicking against the stone. He walked to the door, his footsteps silent on the bare boards. He didn’t ask who it was. Questions like that were for people who expected answers that mattered.

He opened the door.

A woman stood there, backlit by the weak afternoon light from the stairwell window. She was maybe forty, maybe fifty. It was hard to tell. Her face had the closed, weathered look of a ledger whose accounts have all been settled, mostly in red. She wore a dark dress, clean but faded to a colour like dust. In her hands she held a small, shabby suitcase, the kind that carries everything a person owns when everything they own is just evidence of what they’ve lost.

“Leonid Volkov?” Her voice was low, scraped clean of inflection.

“Leo.”

“I was told you find things.”

“Who told you that?”

“A man at the docks. He said you were… thorough.”

Leo leaned against the doorframe. The chill from the stone seeped through his shirt. “I find missing persons. For the authorities. It’s a job.”

“This is not for the authorities.” She didn’t blink. “And he is not missing. I know where he is. I want you to see him.”

“See him.”

“Look at him. Tell me what you see.”

Leo almost smiled. It was a dry, papery feeling. “Madam, I am not a portrait painter. Or a priest.”

“You were an investigator. Before. For the military police.”

“That was a different country.”

“The country is the same. The dirt hasn’t changed.” She shifted the suitcase to her left hand. “He is my brother. He came home six months ago. From the eastern camps. He walks. He eats. He sleeps. He is a… a perfect reconstruction. And he is not my brother.”

A silence settled in the hallway, filled with the distant, damp echo of the city: a tram bell, a shout, the eternal murmur of the river.

“Ghost stories are cheap these days,” Leo said finally. “Everyone has one.”

“This is not a ghost story. It is a taxidermy story. Something has been stuffed into his skin. Something that watches.” She met his eyes. “I will pay you. Not much. But something.”

Leo looked past her, down the stairwell into the building’s dim throat. He thought of the river, of the man mending the net, of the chicory coffee going cold. He had a case file on the small table by the window, a missing husband last seen near the rail yards. A straightforward thing. A thing of this world. He looked back at the woman. Her stillness was absolute. It wasn’t hope that held her there; it was a kind of grim, factual desperation, like a scientist awaiting a result she already knows.

“What’s his name?” Leo asked.

“Anatoly. Anatoly Bragin.”

“And you?”

“Irina.”

“Come in, Irina. Tell me about your stuffed brother.”

The flat was one room, a box of light and dust. A bed, a table, two chairs, a small stove. Books stacked against the wall, not for show but for ballast. Leo poured her the last of the chicory. She sat on the edge of the chair, her back not touching it, the suitcase on the floor between her feet like an anchor.

“He was captured at Korsk,” she began, no preamble. “Winter of ’42. I got two letters, then nothing. For three years, nothing. Then the lists started coming. The living, the dead, the liberated. His name was on a list. ‘Liberated.’ A word like an empty pocket.” She took a sip, didn’t react to the bitterness. “He arrived on a Tuesday. Rain. He stood at the door of our old house—my house now, our parents’ house—with a kitbag and that same look. The reconstruction. He said my name. ‘Irina.’ The right pitch, the right weight. A perfect recording.”

“What did you expect?” Leo asked, lighting a cigarette. “Joy? Tears?”

“I expected a crack,” she said sharply. “A flaw. He was in a camp for three years. Men come back from a week at the front less than they were. He came back… complete. He remembered everything. Childhood games, our mother’s perfume, the name of a dog we had when I was seven. He offered details like a clerk presenting documents.”

“And this is your evidence? That he remembered?”

“He remembered everything, Leo. Not the way memory works. Memory is a sieve. It is a dark room with a faulty lamp. He recited. He presented a flawless biography. When I cried—not for joy, for confusion—he patted my shoulder. Twice. Like a mechanic checking a bolt for tightness.” She set her cup down. “He took his old room. He found work at the printing works, his old job. He goes, he comes back, he eats the food I make, he reads the newspaper. He is a model of reintegration.”

“So he’s adjusted. Some do.”

“He watches the sparrows,” she said, and her voice dropped to a whisper. “On the windowsill. He puts out crumbs. He sits in the chair and he watches them for an hour, two hours. His head tilted. Not with pleasure. With… assessment. One day, a cat from the alley took one. Pounced. There was a flurry, a squeak. Anatoly stood up. He walked to the window. The cat had the sparrow in its mouth. Anatoly looked at the cat, and the cat… it dropped the bird. It just opened its mouth and let it fall, then ran. Anatoly looked at the dead sparrow on the cobbles. He nodded. Once. Then he sat back down and picked up his paper.”

Leo let the smoke drift from his nostrils. The story was strange, but strangeness was the currency of the time. Everyone was trading in it. “Maybe he just hates cats.”

“I followed him,” she said, ignoring him. “Two nights ago. He said he was going to a veterans’ meeting. He does not go to meetings. He went to the old botanical gardens. The greenhouse is shattered, glass everywhere. He stood in the rubble. He wasn’t waiting for anyone. He just… stood. And he took off his glove. He put his bare hand on the stem of a broken plant, something thorny. He pushed his palm onto the thorns. Hard. I saw his shoulders tense. Then he stood there, hand bleeding in the moonlight, for a long time. He put his glove back on and came home. He said the meeting was dull.”

For the first time, Leo felt a small, cold finger trace his spine. Not at the act, but at its purity. It wasn’t madness. Madness had heat, had confusion. This was clinical. A test.

“Why come to me? Why not a doctor? A party official?”

“Doctors make reports. Officials make categories. I do not want him helped or classified. I want him seen. By someone who looks at facts, not souls. You find the missing. I think my brother is missing from himself. I want to know what is in his place.” She bent down, opened the suitcase. Inside were a few clothes, a small cloth bundle. She untied it. On the cloth lay a silver watch, a man’s, old but good. “This was my father’s. It is real silver. It works. It is yours if you come. Just look. Tell me what you see.”

Leo stubbed his cigarette. The watch gleamed dully in the grey light. It was a thing from before. A thing that measured time in a world that believed time had a direction. He thought of the net-mender on the bank, the endless, patient repair of holes. He was being offered a different kind of hole to examine.

“Where does he work?”

“The State Printing Works on Marinsky Street. He leaves at seven, returns at six.”

“I’ll be outside the works at five-thirty tomorrow. Point him out. I’ll follow him. I’ll look. That’s all.”

“That is all I ask.”

She stood, leaving the watch on the table. She didn’t offer her hand. She just picked up her empty suitcase and walked to the door. She paused. “He likes the river, too. He walks there sometimes. He says the water is the only thing that doesn’t pretend to be what it’s not.”

After she left, Leo picked up the watch. It was heavy, solid. He wound it. The tick was soft, insistent. He placed it on the sill next to his cold cup. Down below, the man with the net was gone. The river flowed, a sheet of hammered lead under the gathering dusk. It didn’t care about brothers or sisters or what came back from the camps. It only cared about gravity, about the slow, inevitable pull towards the sea.

Leo put on his coat. He had to go to the rail yards, to ask after a missing husband. A man of flesh and failure, who probably ran off with a waitress or was lying drunk in a ditch. A simple problem. He locked the door behind him, the tick of the silver watch echoing in the empty room, a tiny, mechanical heartbeat in the quiet flat.

---

Chapter 2

CHAPTER 2

The State Printing Works was a soot-blackened monster of brick and blind windows, exhaling a warm, damp breath of ink and machine oil. Leo stood in a doorway across the street, the collar of his coat turned up against the fine, persistent rain that wasn’t quite rain, just the city sweating its damp out of the stones. The shift change was a slow hemorrhage of men. They emerged in twos and threes, their faces grey with fatigue and paper dust, their postures a universal slump. They looked like copies from a worn-out plate, slight variations on a theme of exhaustion.

He saw Irina first. She stood under a leafless linden tree fifty yards down the street, a grey figure in the grey light. She didn’t look at him. Her eyes were fixed on the factory door.

Then he appeared.

Anatoly Bragin was not what Leo had expected. He’d pictured something hollow-eyed, gaunt, a ghost in a uniform. The man who stepped out was solid. Medium height, broad in the shoulder, with a thick neck and close-cropped hair the colour of ash. He moved with a deliberate, efficient grace, like a man who has calculated the minimum effort required for locomotion. He paused on the steps, not to light a cigarette or greet a friend, but to look up at the sky, as if checking a gauge. Then he turned and walked, not with the weary trudge of his comrades, but with a steady, rhythmic pace.

Irina’s head turned slightly, a barely perceptible nod. This was him.

Leo let him get half a block ahead before pushing off from the doorway. He fell into the rhythm of the street, the evening crowd of workers and shoppers, a stream of dark coats. Anatoly didn’t look back. He walked like a man who knows he is not followed, or like a man who does not care.

He did not go home. He walked north, away from the riverine districts, into a neighbourhood of older, grander buildings, pockmarked by shellfire and neglect. He stopped at a small plaza where a baroque fountain stood, dry and filled with rubbish. He didn’t sit. He simply stood before it, looking at the stone nymphs with their chipped faces, their empty urns. Five minutes. Ten. People passed. A woman scolded a child. A tram clanged in the distance. Anatoly was a statue of a man observing a statue.

Then, abruptly, he turned and entered a café.

It was a dim place, called The Green Lamp. The windows were fogged. Leo waited a count of thirty, then went in.

The air was thick with the smell of tobacco, cheap beer, and wet wool. A few men hunched over tables, talking in low tones. Anatoly was at the bar, on a stool. He had a glass of clear liquid in front of him—vodka, likely. He wasn’t drinking it. He was spinning it slowly on the wood, watching the liquid climb the sides but not spill.

Leo took a table in the corner, his back to the wall. He ordered a beer from a tired-looking woman. When it came, he sipped it, watching Anatoly in the smeared mirror behind the bar.

The man’s face was calm, utterly without tension. It was the most unsettling thing about him. In a city of twitches, of nervous glances and forced smiles, Anatoly’s face was a still pond. His eyes, when he glanced up to check the clock, were a pale, flat blue. They took in the information of the clock and then dismissed it.

After twenty minutes of spinning his untouched drink, Anatoly stood. He left a coin on the bar. He walked out.

Leo left a coin of his own and followed.

The rain had hardened into proper drops. Anatoly walked now with purpose, his footsteps tapping a steady rhythm on the wet cobbles. He turned down a narrow alley between two high warehouses, a canyon of dripping brick. Leo hung back at the mouth, peering into the gloom.

Anatoly stopped halfway down. He stood before a section of wall scarred by gunfire, a pockmarked tapestry of some forgotten, minor battle. He reached out and, with his index finger, traced one of the bullet holes. He did it slowly, methodically, as if reading braille. Then he leaned forward and put his eye to it.

He stood there, his body curved against the wall, one eye pressed to a hole that looked into a bricked-up window, for a full two minutes. What did he see? Darkness. Dust. Nothing.

He straightened up. He took a handkerchief from his pocket and wiped the grime from around his eye, a fastidious gesture. Then he continued down the alley and out the other side.

Leo felt a fool. He was following a man who stared at dry fountains and put his eye to blank walls. A simple lunatic, after all. A casualty of the war with a quiet, peculiar fracture. He thought of Irina’s fierce, fearful eyes. The sparrow. The thorns. This was just the mundane theatre of a broken mind.

He almost turned back. But the watch in his pocket felt heavy. And he had promised to look.

On the other side of the alley was a canal, a sluggish, black ribbon of water. A single barge was moored, loaded with timber. Anatoly walked onto the wooden dock. He approached a man who was coiling a rope—a bargee, in heavy boots and a leather apron. Anatoly spoke to him. The man looked up, scowled, shook his head. Anatoly spoke again. He didn’t gesture. He just stood there, his words visible as faint puffs in the cold air. The bargee stopped coiling his rope. He looked at Anatoly differently now. Not with anger, but with a kind of wary confusion. He replied, shorter this time. Anatoly nodded. He reached into his coat pocket and handed the man something. A small, dark object. The bargee took it, looked at it in his palm, then quickly closed his fist. He gave one sharp nod.

Anatoly turned and walked away, back the way he had come.

Leo pressed himself into a doorway as Anatoly passed the alley mouth. He didn’t look in. His face was still that placid mask. He walked past, back towards the main thoroughfare, towards home, presumably.

Leo waited, then walked onto the dock. The bargee saw him coming and busied himself with the rope again, his shoulders tense.

“Evening,” Leo said.

The man grunted.

“Friend of yours?” Leo nodded in the direction Anatoly had gone.

“No friend.”

“Looked like you were having a chat.”

“Man asked about the timber. Where it’s from. Where it’s going. Idle talk.”

“He paid you for idle talk?”

The bargee’s eyes flickered. “Who are you?”

“Just a man who’s curious. What did he give you?”

“Piss off.”

Leo took out his wallet, showed the man his old military police identification. It was expired, meaningless, but the shape of the authority still clung to it like a smell. The bargee’s defiance wilted. He sighed, opened his fist.

In his palm lay a small, perfect piece of coal. Not a lump, but a fragment that had been worked, polished into a smooth, obsidian oval. It caught the wan light dully.

“He gave you coal.”

“He asked about the weight of the load. The draft of the barge. How deep the canal is here. Then he gave me this. Said it was for my time. Said it was… a good piece. Burns long.” The bargee sounded bewildered. “Who carries polished coal in his pocket?”

“What did you tell him? About the depth?”

“Told him what anyone knows. It’s silted up. Four feet, maybe, at the centre. Less near the edges. Useless for proper barges. Why?”

Leo looked at the black water. Four feet. He looked back at the piece of coal. “Keep it,” he said. “Maybe it does burn long.”

He walked away, leaving the bargee staring at the strange payment in his hand.

The rain was steady now. Leo walked without direction, his mind working. The actions were not those of a madman. A madman’s behaviour is a language of its own, a syntax of chaos. Anatoly’s actions had a grammar. Observation. Test. Exchange. The fountain (aesthetic observation?). The bullet hole (historical observation?). The bargee (practical measurement?). And the tests: the thorns, the sparrow. Testing pain, testing fear, testing cause and effect.

He was not remembering. He was cataloguing.

Leo found himself at the river again. It was full dark now, the water a black expanse punctured by the wavering reflections of the few working streetlights on the far bank. He stood where the net-mender had been. He thought of Irina’s words: Something has been stuffed into his skin.

A shape detached itself from the shadows under the bridge. Irina.

“You followed him,” she said. It wasn’t a question.

“Yes.”

“And?”

“He looked at a dry fountain. He put his eye to a bullet hole. He asked a bargee about the depth of the canal and paid him with a piece of polished coal.”

She was silent for a moment. The rain pattered on her shawl. “You see? It is not madness. It is… research.”

“What does he do at the printing works? What is his job?”

“He is a typesetter. He arranges the letters. The lead pieces.”

A typesetter. Assembling reality from small, hard pieces of metal. “Has he brought anything home? Strange things?”

“No. Nothing. He is clean. Tidy. He leaves no trace.” She wrapped her arms around herself. “What do I do, Leo?”

“You live with him. You watch the sparrows with him.”

“That is no answer.”

“It’s the only answer there is. He’s broken, Irina. In a new way. A quiet way. The war made many new kinds of broken.” Even as he said it, the words felt inadequate, a blanket thrown over a shape that didn’t fit.

“He is not broken. Broken things have pieces. He is whole. He is a closed circle.” She looked out at the river, her profile sharp against the gloom. “Will you come? To the house? See him in his… habitat?”

Leo thought of the warm, silent flat, the books, the ticking watch. He thought of the simple case of the missing husband. He nodded. “Tomorrow evening. I’ll come as a friend of a friend. Looking for work at the printing works.”

“He will see through you.”

“Probably. But we’ll play the game.”

She gave another of her sharp nods and melted back into the shadows, becoming just another part of the night.

Leo lit a cigarette, the match flaring briefly in the damp. On the black water, a piece of debris floated past—a shattered crate, perhaps. It turned slowly in the current, a dark shape on a darker sea, moving with the river’s ancient, mindless pull towards an indifferent destination. He watched it until it disappeared, feeling the polished coal of Anatoly’s mystery sitting in his own mind, hard, black, and utterly inscrutable.

---

Chapter 3

CHAPTER 3

The house was a pre-war phantom, a narrow, three-story structure squeezed between taller, bleaker buildings. The façade had lost most of its plaster, revealing the brick beneath like bones after a famine. One window on the second floor was lit, a yellow square in the gathering dusk. The others were dark, sightless.

Irina opened the door before Leo could knock. She had changed into a different dress, equally dark, equally worn. She didn’t speak, just stepped back to let him in. The hallway was cold and smelled of boiled cabbage, damp wool, and the faint, metallic tang of the printing works that clung to Anatoly.

“He’s in the kitchen,” she murmured. “I told him an old comrade of our cousin’s was coming. A man down on his luck, seeking a reference for the works. He said he would listen.”

“How did he say it?”

She understood. “He said, ‘I will evaluate his claim.’ Those words. Evaluate.”

The kitchen was at the back of the house, overlooking a tiny, bricked yard. Anatoly sat at a plain wooden table, a newspaper open before him. He was not reading it. He was looking at the back page, which held a large, grainy photograph of a new tractor factory under construction. A headline proclaimed PROGRESS.

“Anatoly,” Irina said, her voice carefully neutral. “This is Leo. I told you.”

Anatoly looked up. His eyes moved to Leo, not quickly, not slowly. They were the pale, assessing eyes Leo had seen in the café mirror. They took in Leo’s coat, his shoes, the line of his jaw, the set of his shoulders. It was not a social glance. It was an appraisal.

“Leo,” Anatoly said. The name in his mouth was just a word, a label. He did not smile. He gestured to a chair. “Sit. Irina says you seek work.”

Leo sat, placing his worn cap on the table. “I do. Heard the works might need a night watchman. Or a loader. I have references.” He slid a folded paper across the table, a forgery he’d used before.

Anatoly did not touch it. He looked at it, then back at Leo. “Your hands are not a loader’s hands. The knuckles are not enlarged, the palms are calloused but in the wrong places. A watchman perhaps. But your posture is not that of a man used to stillness. You are a man who moves. An investigator, perhaps. Or a soldier.”

The air in the kitchen tightened. Irina, standing by the stove, went very still.

Leo kept his face relaxed. “I was. Military police. Now I’m not. Now I need work.”

“Military police,” Anatoly repeated. He folded the newspaper with precise, sharp creases, aligning the edges perfectly. The photograph of the tractor factory vanished. “You are used to evaluating truth. To interrogation.”

“I’m used to filling out forms. Mostly.”

“Forms are a type of truth. A limited one.” Anatoly leaned forward slightly. “Why come to me? I am a typesetter. I have no influence.”

“Your sister is a kind woman. She thought you might have… insight.”

Anatoly’s gaze flickered to Irina, just for an instant. There was no warmth in it, no accusation. It was like a beam of light passing over a piece of furniture. “Insight. Yes. The works functions on routine. On precision. The letters must be arranged in the correct order. A single ‘p’ where a ‘b’ should be renders the message false. Truth is a matter of correct arrangement.” He spoke as if lecturing. “A watchman’s truth is the truth of absence. Nothing missing, nothing out of place. Can you perceive absence, Leo?”

It was an odd question. Philosophical, almost. But there was no philosophy in Anatoly’s eyes, only a flat curiosity.

“I can perceive when something’s where it shouldn’t be,” Leo said. “That’s usually the problem.”

“Indeed.” Anatoly stood up abruptly. “Come. I will show you my work. It may help you understand the environment.”

He walked out of the kitchen without checking if Leo followed. Irina’s eyes met Leo’s, wide with a silent warning.

Anatoly led him up two flights of narrow stairs to a small room at the front of the house. His room. It was monastic. A narrow bed, perfectly made. A small desk. A single bookshelf holding not books, but rows of identical, grey notebooks. On the desk was a reading lamp, a pen, and an open notebook. The window looked out over the street.

“Sit,” Anatoly said, pointing to the bed.

Leo sat. The room was cold, cleaner than any room had a right to be. It smelled of soap and paper.

Anatoly remained standing by the window, his back to Leo. “The printing works is a universe of noise and vibration. The great presses shake the floor. The men shout. But in the typesetting room, there is a different sound. The click of lead. The whisper of the galleys. It is a world of small, hard facts.” He turned around. He held something in his hand. One of the lead type pieces. A letter ‘A’. “This is a fact. It is not an ‘A’. It is the reverse of an ‘A’. It is a mould. It exists to create its opposite. To make a mark. Its truth is in its function.”

He placed the piece of type on the desk with a soft click.

“You followed me yesterday,” he said, his tone not changing at all.

The statement lay in the cold air between them, simple and solid as the piece of lead.

Leo didn’t bother to deny it. “I did.”

“Why?”

“Your sister is worried about you.”

“Worry is an emotion. It is based on a perception of threat or anomaly. What anomaly does she perceive?”

“She thinks you came back… different.”

“I am different. I was gone for three years, seven months, and fourteen days. Change is inevitable. It is a fact.” He picked up the notebook from his desk. “I am documenting the changes. In the city. In people. In myself. It is a project.”

“Documenting? By staring at fountains? By putting your eye to bullet holes?”

Anatoly opened the notebook. He showed a page to Leo. It was covered in small, precise handwriting. At the top was a heading: Canal Sector 7-G, Depth & Traffic. Below were notes on the barge, the timber, the silt depth. A diagram. At the bottom: Subject: Bargee (unidentified). Payment rendered: carbon sample. Reaction: confusion, then acceptance. Note: human capacity to integrate incongruity is high.

“You’re studying us,” Leo said, a chill spreading from his core.

“I am recording. The world is a text that has been badly damaged. I am trying to… reset the type. To see the original layout.” He closed the notebook. “Your presence is a new variable. Irina’s worry introduced you. You are a man who looks for what is missing. What do you think is missing from me, Leo?”

The question was devastating in its directness. Leo looked at the man’s placid face, the cool eyes. He thought of the thorns, the sparrow, the coal.

“Feeling,” Leo said quietly.

Anatoly considered this. He nodded, as if Leo had confirmed a hypothesis. “Feeling is a biochemical signal. A response to stimulus. I have the responses. I feel hunger. Fatigue. Cold.” He walked to the window. “When I press my hand to thorns, I register pain. The signal is clear. But the… commentary is absent. The ‘why’ of the pain. The protest.” He looked back at Leo. “Is that what is missing? The commentary?”

“That’s what makes us human.”

“Is it?” Anatoly asked, genuinely curious. “Or is it what makes us inefficient? The commentary is what leads to despair. To hope. To error. The camp was a place without commentary. Only fact. Cold. Hunger. Work. Death. It was a very… clear world.”

“And you miss that clarity.”

“I am that clarity.” He said it without pride, without sadness. As a fact. “Irina’s commentary tells her I am wrong. Broken. Your commentary will tell you I am a subject of interest. But I am simply here. A fact among facts.”

Footsteps on the stairs. Irina appeared in the doorway, her face pale. “Supper is ready.”

Anatoly smiled. It was a perfect muscular contraction, lifting the corners of his mouth, crinkling the edges of his eyes. A flawless imitation. “Good. We will eat.”

The meal was a silent affair. A thin stew, black bread. Anatoly ate with the same efficient movements. He did not slurp. He did not rush. He consumed exactly what was before him. Irina pushed her food around her bowl. Leo ate, his mind racing.

He had come to see a man, and he had seen one. But he had also seen the outline of something else, something that lived inside the man like a hermit crab in a borrowed shell, studying the world from behind the eyes of its host. It wasn’t possession. It was something colder. A succession.

As Leo was putting on his coat to leave, Anatoly approached him at the door. Irina was clearing the dishes, the clatter of crockery loud in the quiet house.

“You will continue to follow me,” Anatoly stated.

“Will I?”

“It is your function. To look for the missing. You will look at me and try to find what Irina says is not there. It is an interesting experiment. I will not alter my behaviour. For the integrity of the study.” He reached into his pocket and held out his hand. On his palm were two more pieces of polished coal. “For your time.”

Leo looked at the black, gleaming stones. He took them. They were cold, surprisingly heavy. “What do you get from this… study?”

“Data,” Anatoly said. “Understanding. When you know how a thing works, you know what it will do. The world becomes… predictable.” He gave that perfect, empty smile again. “Goodnight, Investigator.”

Leo walked out into the night. The pieces of coal sat in his coat pocket, knocking together with a soft, clacking sound as he walked. He thought of the lead type, the reverse ‘A’, existing only to create its opposite. He thought of a man who was a mould for humanity, pressing himself against the world to see what mark he would make.

He was at the river again. He took the two pieces of coal and threw them, one after the other, into the black water. They vanished without a sound. No splash. The river absorbed them, as it absorbed everything.

But in his mind, he saw Anatoly’s flat, pale eyes. The study had begun. And Leo realized, with a sinking certainty, that he was no longer just the observer. He had become a specimen under glass, his own reactions now part of the data, a variable in the quiet, terrible experiment of a man who had left his commentary in a camp three years, seven months, and fourteen days ago. The stakes were no longer just a sister’s peace of mind. They were the definition of the thing watching from the window, learning how to be, piece by piece, fact by fact, until the reconstruction was complete. And what would it do then, when it finally understood the text?



---

Chapter 4

CHAPTER 4

The city in the morning was a palimpsest. The present—the new trams with their shrieking brakes, the women in factory overalls hurrying across the squares—was written thinly over the older, darker text. The pockmarks of shrapnel in the limestone façades. The way people still walked close to the walls, as if expecting a sudden downpour from a clear sky. Leo walked through it, the two pieces of coal gone but their phantom weight still in his pocket. He was a man carrying an absence.

He found Karel at the usual place, a basement archive that smelled of damp paper and glue. The old historian was a creature of the submerged text, a scholar of the city’s erased footnotes. He looked up from a sheaf of personnel records, his glasses perched on the end of his nose. He didn’t ask about the visit to Anatoly. He read it in the set of Leo’s shoulders, in the careful neutrality of his face.

“You have that look,” Karel said, closing a folder. “The look of a man who has seen a perfect machine and is wondering where to insert the crowbar.”

“I need a location,” Leo said, his voice flat. “A specific one. Not on any current map.”

Karel took off his glasses, polished them on his sleeve. His movements were like the net-mender’s by the river: small, necessary, correcting an unseen flaw. “Names change. Grid references are redrawn. But the ground remembers. What are we looking for?”

“A camp. Administrative, not frontline. Designation ‘Station Kappa’. Three years, seven months, and fourteen days ago, it ceased to function.”

Karel’s hands stilled. The basement seemed to grow colder, the damp rising from the stone floor. “Kappa,” he repeated, the word a soft exhalation. “They were all Greek letters, weren’t they? Clean, theoretical. Alpha, Beta, Gamma… Kappa. I have seen fragments. Logistics reports. Calorie allocations per unit. Mortality projections.” He looked at Leo, his eyes old and tired. “You don’t find a place like that to bring flowers, Leo.”

“I need to see the text,” Leo said. “The original. Not the commentary.”

Karel was silent for a long moment. Then he moved, a sudden, decisive uncurling. He went to a steel cabinet, its lock already broken, and pulled out a rolled map. It crackled as he spread it on the table, weighting its corners with books. It was a pre-war topographic survey, the paper yellowing at the folds. His finger traced north-east, following the river’s tributary like a vein. It stopped in a region of dense, printed green. “Here. The forest swallowed it. The roads were logging tracks, seasonal. They built it there because it was cheap, and because the trees muffled sound. And because things lost in a forest stay lost.”

Leo studied the contours. It was a cup of land, a shallow bowl held by hills. A place things could settle in and stay. “How do we get in?”

“We?” Karel gave a dry, papery cough that might have been a laugh. “I am a librarian of the disappeared. I do not visit the pages in person.”

“I can’t read the terrain alone. I need a guide. Someone who knows that forest.”

Karel looked at him, then slowly nodded. He went to a small desk, scribbled a name and an address on a scrap of paper. “Marta. She was a forester’s daughter. She… knows the old paths. The ones that don’t forgive mistakes.” He handed Leo the paper. “Tell her I sent you. And that the book is still open.”
  - Marta lived in a tenement on the city’s northern edge, where the cobblestones gave way to rutted dirt and the air smelled of wet earth and woodsmoke. She answered the door wearing men’s trousers and a thick wool sweater, her hair tied back in a severe knot. Her eyes were the colour of river silt, and they assessed Leo without welcome.

“Karel’s man,” she said, not a question. She’d been expecting him, or someone like him. The war had created a type, and Leo wore its uniform: the worn coat, the watchful stillness, the smell of old smoke and unresolved questions.

“He said you know the woods east of the Vrana tributary.”

“I know them.” She didn’t invite him in. “Why?”

“I need to get to a place that was there. And then get out.”

A flicker in her eyes, a minute tightening at the corner of her mouth. She knew. Of course she knew. The forest had its own gossip, carried on the wind, seeped into the roots. “Station Kappa,” she said, the words hanging in the cold air between them. “Nothing there now. Stones. Rust. Bad memories.”

“I need to see the nothing.”

She looked past him, towards the line of trees that began a hundred yards from her doorstep. They were bare, black branches scratching at a low grey sky. “It is two days’ walk. The bridges are gone. The river must be crossed at the ford, and the water is high with melt. The approach… the ground is not stable. It remembers things.” She looked back at him. “You will pay. In real currency. And you will do exactly as I say. In there, a wrong step is not a mistake. It is an answer. And the forest always asks the question.”

“Agreed.”

“We leave at first light. Bring food for three days. A knife. No flashlight—the batteries die, and the light is a confession. We move in the grey times. Dawn and dusk.” She finally stepped back, a tacit invitation over the threshold. “You can sleep by the stove. Don’t touch anything.”
  - The ford was not a place, but an argument with the river. The water, grey and muscular, fought the stones, throwing up a cold spray that soaked them to the skin before they were halfway across. It was deeper than Marta had hoped, the current grabbing at their knees with icy hands. Leo carried his pack high, feeling the suck of the riverbed mud through his boots. Each step was a calculation against the push of the water. He thought of the coal, sinking without a sound into a darkness just like this. The river was taking things from him again.

Marta moved ahead, sure-footed as a deer, using a long staff to probe the unseen bottom. She didn’t look back. Her trust was not in him, but in his instinct for self-preservation. On the far bank, she waited, steam rising from her shoulders in the chill air. She didn’t offer congratulations. Survival was not an achievement; it was a temporary condition.

“From here,” she said, her voice low, “the forest decides. Stay close. Step where I step.”

The woods closed around them like a church. The light filtered down in dusty, slanting columns, failing to reach the floor where a thick carpet of old needles and decaying leaves swallowed all sound. Their footsteps were whispers. The air was still and cold, smelling of pine resin and rot. It was a world of vertical lines and deep silence.

Marta set a punishing pace, following no path Leo could see. She navigated by the slant of the light on the moss, by the particular crook of a lightning-blasted birch, by a language of signs written in the landscape. Leo followed, his city senses dull and useless. The silence was a pressure. It wasn’t an absence of noise, but a presence. It listened.

They stopped as the last of the grey daylight bled from the sky. Marta chose a spot under the spreading roots of a fallen spruce, a natural hollow lined with dry needles. She built no fire. They ate cold sausage and hard bread, the sounds of their chewing absurdly loud.

“Why do you do this?” Leo asked, his voice a intrusion. “Guide people like me.”

Marta took a slow drink from her canteen. “The forest is full of ghosts. Most are memories. Some are men who got lost and never admitted it. They wander, looking for a path that doesn’t exist. They blunder. They break things.” She looked at him through the gloom. “I shepherd the ghosts. Keep them from doing more harm. And sometimes, I lead them to what they’re looking for, so they can see it is just a hole in the ground, and leave.”

“And Kappa? Is it just a hole in the ground?”

She was silent for so long he thought she wouldn’t answer. “No,” she said finally. “Some holes are shaped like a question. You fall in, and you find you are the answer.” She rolled onto her side, her back to him. The conversation was over.

Leo lay awake, watching the black branches weave against a sky that held no stars. The forest’s silence was different now. It felt like waiting. He thought of Anatoly’s flat eyes, the polished coal. The man was a theorist, reconstructing a model of humanity from first principles. But here, in this ancient, indifferent wood, theory met texture. What data could this place provide? What would a man learn from these stones, these silent trees? The past was not a lesson. It was a soil. And things grew in it, whether you planted them or not.
  - The second day’s travel was through a deeper, older part of the forest. The trees were giants, their trunks furrowed like wise, terrible faces. The ground began to slope upwards, a long, gradual rise towards a ridge. The silence grew heavier, more attentive.

Marta slowed, her movements becoming even more deliberate. She pointed to the ground. At first, Leo saw nothing. Then he made it out: a faint, linear depression running through the undergrowth, parallel to their path. Not a natural contour. A road. Overgrown, reclaimed, but stubborn in its geometry.

“The service road,” she murmured, barely a breath. “We follow its edge. Not on it. Never on it.”

They were shadows flitting beside a ghost. Leo began to see other shapes in the green: a squared-off hummock that was too regular to be a termite mound—a collapsed bunker. A gleam of rusty iron half-buried in leaf mould—a piece of machinery. The forest was digesting it all, slowly, patiently. But it hadn’t finished. The artefacts of purpose persisted, obscene in this place of wild, unthinking growth.

Near midday, Marta froze. She held up a clenched fist. Leo stopped, his breath loud in his ears. She pointed.

Ahead, between the trees, was a fence. Not wood, but metal, though so thick with rust it looked like a blood-red scar through the greenery. It was twisted and sagging, vines pulling it towards the earth. Beyond it, the trees were thinner, younger. A field of saplings and bramble filled a vast clearing.

Station Kappa.

They didn’t approach the fence. Marta led them on a wide, cautious arc, climbing the ridge that formed the northern rim of the bowl. From there, looking down, the full shape of it was revealed.

It was a negative space. The long, low outlines of barracks were just faint, rectangular depressions in the earth, like graves for giants. A concrete pad, webbed with cracks, marked what might have been a kitchen or an administration block. At the centre, a single chimney stack of soot-blackened brick thrust up from the brambles, a stark, declarative finger against the sky. The place was a mould, just as Anatoly had said. But the cast had been taken, the sculpture removed, and only the hollow form remained, filling slowly with rain and wind and time.

It was utterly, profoundly empty. And yet, Leo felt a gaze upon him. Not from the clearing, but from the idea of it. The past was not here. The past was the future this place had predicted, the future that had arrived and now lived in a neat flat, conducting studies. This was just the discarded shell.

“We will camp here tonight,” Marta said, her voice hushed. “On the high ground. You can go down at first light. One hour. No more. I will not come with you.”

“Why not?”

She looked at the clearing below, her face stone. “Because I know what questions that hole asks. And I do not want to know my answers.” She turned away to make camp, a finality in her movements.

Leo sat on a fallen log, watching the shadows lengthen across the bowl. The sinking certainty he’d felt at the river crystallized into a cold, hard knowledge. Anatoly hadn’t sent him here. Leo had come of his own will, following a trail of clues the man had left like breadcrumbs. He was performing exactly as predicted. His curiosity, his need to see the source, his very presence here—it was all data. He was inside the experiment, reacting to the stimuli. The thought was a cage.

As dusk settled, a fog began to rise from the low ground of the clearing. It pooled in the depressions where the barracks had been, then spilled out, a slow, silent tide of white. It swallowed the chimney stack, then the brambles, until the entire bowl was a lake of cloud, pristine and still. It was beautiful, and it was a lie. It made the horror beneath look soft, clean, erased.

Marta handed him a piece of hard cheese. “The fog is the forest breathing out what it does not want to remember,” she said. “By morning, it will be gone. The truth will be visible again. It always is.”

They ate in silence. The temperature dropped. The fog below glowed faintly with the last of the reflected light, a false moonlit sea.

“What will you do,” Marta asked, “when you have seen it? When you have your… text?”

Leo stared into the white void. “I don’t know. Confront the commentary, I suppose.”

She shook her head, a small, sharp movement. “You cannot confront a reflection in a mirror. You can only break the glass, and then you have a thousand fragments, each showing a different piece of the same thing.” She wrapped her blanket tighter around herself. “Sleep. Your hour comes early.”

But Leo didn’t sleep. He watched the fog. He thought of Anatoly’s study, of being a specimen under glass. And he realized the most terrible thing of all: the man in the flat was not the only one conducting a study. This place, this hole in the world, was also an experiment. It asked: what grows in the soil we prepared? What walks out of the forest when the work is done? It had produced its result. Anatoly was the harvest.

And Leo, by coming here, was just another data point. A control subject. The man who looked at the empty field and felt dread. What would Anatoly learn from that?
  - First light came as a grey seep into the sky. The fog was gone, as Marta had promised. The clearing lay exposed, wet and glistening, sharp-edged in the dawn.

Marta nodded to him. “One hour. The sun touches that pine top,” she pointed to a distant tree on the eastern ridge, “you return. Or I leave without you.”

Leo descended the slope. The ground was soft, sucking at his boots. He passed the rusted fence, stepping over a collapsed section. The silence here was different from the forest’s. It was a vacuum, a held breath.

He walked to the centre, to the chimney stack. Up close, the bricks were stained with dark, vertical streaks. He placed a hand against them. They were cold, colder than the morning air. He looked around at the sea of brambles, the depressions. He tried to feel something—horror, grief, anger. He felt only a vast, echoing neutrality. The river’s indifference, made land.

He was about to turn back when a glint caught his eye. Not rust. Something smoother. At the base of the chimney, half-hidden in a nettle patch, was a fragment of glass. He knelt, brushed the leaves aside. It was a lens, thick, the edge ground smooth. A magnifying glass, or part of one. The handle was gone. He picked it up. It was heavy, solid. He held it up, and through the warped, dirty glass, the world distorted. The chimney stretched like a black tongue. The sky bent.

He put it in his pocket. It was not a text. It was a tool for looking.

He took one last survey of the clearing. His eye traced the lines of the foundations, the logic of the place. Administration here. Barracks there. The chimney at the centre. And there, at the far western edge, another, smaller concrete pad. He hadn’t noticed it before. He began walking towards it, his hour almost up.

This pad had a feature. A metal hatch, rusted shut, set into the concrete at an angle. A root had grown under its edge, prizing it open a crack. It was too narrow to see into. Leo took out his knife, wedged it into the crack, and levered. The rust screamed, a shocking sound in the silence. The hatch gave an inch, then stuck fast.

But it was enough. He knelt, and through the narrow opening, he saw only darkness. And then, as his eyes adjusted, he saw steps leading down. A cellar. Or a bunker.

And at the bottom of the steps, just visible in the sliver of light from the hatch, was a shape. Not a root, not a stone. A shape with a familiar, terrible geometry. The curve of a skull. The long lines of bones, still sheathed in the dark cloth of a uniform.

He had found a piece of the text that had not been erased. A primary source. Not a commentary, but a raw, unprocessed datum.

The sun touched the top of the pine tree on the eastern ridge.

He heard Marta’s whistle, sharp and impatient, from the hillside.

Leo stood, his heart a cold, hard drum in his chest. He looked from the hatch to the ridge and back. He was out of time. The specimen had discovered something not in the study’s design. A variable. An anomaly.

He turned and began to run back towards the tree line, towards Marta. The lens and the bones burned in his mind, twin pieces of evidence. One for seeing. One for being.

The experiment was no longer clean. He had contaminated it. And as he scrambled up the slope, he knew, with a certainty that was almost relief, that Anatoly would have to account for that. The observer had altered the observed. The reflection had just cracked the glass.

From the ridge, he looked back once. The clearing lay inert, silent. The hatch was a dark tooth in the grey concrete. It was open. It was a question he now carried with him.

And he knew, as Marta turned and led him back into the waiting trees, that the study had entered a new, unpredictable phase. The thing in the flat was about to receive a data point it could not explain. And Leo had just become something far more dangerous than a specimen.

He had become an error.



---

Chapter 5

CHAPTER 5

The forest didn’t care that the study was compromised. It absorbed Leo’s frantic return the same way it had absorbed the thud of his boots when he was a specimen following a protocol, a thing to be measured and logged. Green, deep, indifferent. Marta marched ahead of him, her silence a thicker wood than the one they moved through. She didn’t ask. He didn’t offer. The facts were a cold weight in his pocket—the lens—and a colder geometry in his mind—the bones. They walked not as hunter and specimen, but as two carriers of the same contagion. The air between them hummed with the unsaid.

The flat, when they reached it, was a cage of stale light. Anatoly was at the table, but he wasn’t working. He was waiting. His hands were flat on the wood, fingers splayed like a man trying to keep a surface from tilting. The thing in the flat had been fed their return, the disruption in the schedule. It had calculated the anomaly.

“The morning transect was incomplete,” Anatoly said. His voice was a clinical instrument, but Leo heard the strain at its edges, the high, thin whine of a saw binding in green wood.

“I found something,” Leo said. His own voice surprised him. It was the river’s voice. Grey, muscular.

“The protocol is the finding. The data set is curated. You are the instrument. Instruments do not find. They record.” Anatoly’s eyes were on Marta. A query. She gave a minute shake of her head. Not my intervention. His.

Leo reached into his pocket. He did not slam the lens on the table. He placed it. A soft, definitive click. The brass cylinder lay between them like a spent cartridge.

Anatoly did not touch it. He looked at it as if it were a dead insect, a piece of irrelevant grit. “A relic. The forest is full of them. This is not data. This is noise.”

“It was concealed. Deliberately. In the stump at grid seven.” Leo leaned forward, his palms on the table, mirroring Anatoly’s posture. Observer and observed, reflecting each other. “And there’s a hatch. Rusted shut. I opened it.”

A flicker in Anatoly’s face. A tic beneath his left eye. A variable entering his equation. “Irrelevant. The study parameters are the clearing, the flora, the atmospheric particulates. Not archaeology.”

“At the bottom of the steps,” Leo continued, the words coming now like stones dropped into a well, each one falling a long way before it hit, “there’s a body. In a uniform. Our uniform.”

The silence that followed was not empty. It was a pressurized vessel. Leo watched the words work on Anatoly. They did not shock him. They corroded him. They ate at the clean lines of the study, at the fiction of the controlled environment. They were a return of the repressed, a term Leo had read in one of the censored texts, a ghost from a theoretical past.

“You contaminated the site.” Anatoly finally said. It was an accusation, but it was also a plea. A wish for the problem to be procedural, not fundamental.

“The site was already contaminated,” Leo said. “By whoever put him there. By whoever hid that lens. Your study is built on a grave you didn’t bother to survey.”

Anatoly stood up. He was not a tall man, but he drew himself up into a shape of authority, a silhouette from the old world. “You will return to your cell. You will be debriefed. The data from this morning will be excised. The transect will be re-run tomorrow with a new observer.”

“Marta?” Leo asked, though he knew.

“Marta has demonstrated compromised judgement. She will be reassigned.”

Marta did not move. Her face was a carved thing. Leo saw it then—the paring down. She was being whittled to her core, and the process was not new to her.

This was the confrontation. Not with a gun or a fist, but with the architecture of his captivity. The greatest enemy was not the man, but the man’s idea, the pristine, heartless logic of the study. Leo felt his fear then, cold and specific. Not fear of pain, but of erasure. Of being rendered back into noise.

“No,” Leo said.

The word was small. It was a pebble. But in the flat, it was a detonation.

Anatoly blinked. “You are refusing a directive?”

“I am refusing to be an instrument. You wanted to see what a man becomes when you strip away his context. When you make him a specimen. Well, look.” Leo spread his hands. “He becomes a man who finds things. Who asks questions. Who says no. Your experiment is over. It ended the moment I opened that hatch.”

Anatoly’s hand went to the drawer of the table. It was a smooth, practiced movement. When it came out, it held a pistol. It was a small, ugly thing, the blueing worn from its grip. A tool for putting down dogs. Or errors.

“The study continues,” Anatoly said. His voice had regained its flatness. The variable was to be deleted. The protocol restored. “You will return to your cell. Now.”

Leo looked at the gun. It was, in its way, honest. It was the final argument of every system he had ever known. He had faced it before, in other hands, in other uniforms. His fear condensed, hardened into a kind of clarity. This was the defeat. The symbolic death. Not to be killed, but to be marched back into the silence, the data of his discovery scrubbed, himself reduced once more to a set of inputs and outputs. To be made a specimen again, after glimpsing what it was to be a witness.

He took a step back. Not towards the door to his cell, but towards the window. The river was out there, moving. The man with the net was probably still there, mending his small, necessary failures.

“You can shoot me,” Leo said. “But the hatch is open. Marta has seen it. You can’t shoot the forest. You can’t shoot the bones. The data is loose.”

“Marta is a loyal citizen,” Anatoly said, but the gun’s muzzle drifted, just a fraction, to include her in its arc.

In that fractional drift, Leo saw the crack in the glass. The observer, terrified of the observed, was now observing his own instrument as a threat. The system was consuming itself.

Marta spoke, her voice a dry rustle. “The lens is pre-war. Military surveyor’s issue. The uniform in the hatch is the same.” She was not looking at Anatoly. She was looking at the lens on the table, as if reading a text from it. “Who was studying this place before you, Anatoly? And what did they find that required a body in a bunker?”

It was the question Leo carried. Marta had taken its weight from him. She had chosen her side. Not for him, but against the lie.

Anatoly’s face paled. The gun was now a problem. It was a datum he could not explain. He could not shoot them both and maintain the fiction of the study. The report would be impossible to write. The thing in the flat would demand an explanation for the terminal noise.

Leo saw his moment. It was not a surge of strength, but an insight, cold and sharp as the lens’s glass. His reason to fight was not hope. It was not freedom. It was legacy. The man in the bunker had been erased. His story had been buried. Leo had been brought here to be erased in a different way, his story replaced with data points. This was the war, continued by other means. The war against memory. The war against the specific, messy, human fact in favor of the clean, general lie.

He was not a specimen. He was a successor.

“You’re right, Anatoly,” Leo said, his voice dropping to a conversational tone. “The past is a foreign country. But it seems they left behind some unfinished business. And we’re the inheritors. You can try to bury it again. But you’ll have to bury us with it. And then who will run your transects? Who will you observe? The ghosts?”

He took a step towards the table. Towards the gun.

Anatoly’s finger tightened on the trigger. But his eyes were on Marta, who had not moved, who was a statue of judgement. He was alone in his logic. The system had no allies, only components. And two of his components had just achieved consciousness.

With a sound that was half sob, half snarl, Anatoly lowered the gun. He did not put it down. He let it hang at his side, a useless weight. He had been defeated not by force, but by epistemology. The observed had altered the observer.

“What do you want?” he whispered. The question was the white flag.

“The truth,” Leo said. “Not your truth. The one under the ground. We go back. Now. All of us. We document what is in that bunker. You will file it as a supplementary discovery within your study parameters. An unexpected subsurface anomaly. That’s all your masters need to know. And we will know the rest.”

It was a bargain. A way for Anatoly to survive, to keep his study, his flat, his life. A way to incorporate the anomaly. To control the narrative of its discovery.

Anatoly’s shoulders slumped. The paring down was happening to him now. The hard, useless core of him was fear, and Leo saw it, naked and pulsing. He nodded, once.

The victory tasted of chalk and chicory. It was not freedom. It was a deeper entanglement. But it was Leo’s entanglement, on his terms. He had moved from object to subject. From error to author.
  - The three of them stood at the edge of the clearing as the afternoon light bled out of the sky. The hatch was a black mouth. Anatoly held a powerful electric torch, its beam a trembling blade in the growing dusk. He had brought a camera, a notebook. The archivist of the unwanted past.

Leo went first. The steps were concrete, slick with condensed damp. The air that rose was cold and smelled of earth and something else—a faint, acrid tang of chemical decay, of old wool and older death. The torch beam swept down, finding the shape.

It was exactly as he had seen it, and entirely different. The uniform was dark green, the leather of the boots cracked like dry mud. The body lay on its side, curled slightly, as if in sleep. One arm was outstretched, the fingers of the hand mere bones, seeming to point towards the steps. Towards the way out he never found.

But it was the skull that held them. It was turned upwards, the jaw agape in a silent, permanent shout. And nestled in the dark hollow of the left eye socket, catching Anatoly’s torch beam with a sickly green glint, was a second lens.

Anatoly made a sound, a choked inhalation. He pushed past Leo, his professional detachment shattered. He knelt beside the skeleton, not with reverence, but with a frantic, greedy need. He did not touch the lens in the eye. He shone his light around the small, cramped space.

The bunker was not empty. Against the far wall were crates, their wood swollen and splitting. One had fallen open. Inside, nestled in straw that had become a damp mulch, were more brass cylinders. Dozens of them. And beside the crates, a small, rusted table held a notebook, its cover mottled with mold.

Leo watched Anatoly. This was the reward. Not a key to a cell, but a key to a deeper lock. The past had not been erased. It had been filed away. Hidden by someone, for someone. A primary source, waiting.

Anatoly reached for the notebook. His hands, which had been so steady with a pistol, trembled as he peeled back the cover. The pages were fused, but the first one came free with a soft, tearing sigh. He held it in the beam of light.

His lips moved, reading. The color drained from his face completely, leaving him the grey of the concrete around them.

“What is it?” Marta asked from the steps, her voice echoing in the tomb.

Anatoly did not answer. He looked up, his eyes finding Leo’s. In them was no triumph, no scholarly excitement. There was only a horror so profound it had emptied him. He was not looking at a colleague or a specimen. He was looking at a prophecy fulfilled.

He held out the page.

Leo took it. The paper was fragile, the ink blurred but legible. It was not a personal diary. It was a log. A series of entries, dates, coordinates. And a title, neatly centered at the top of the sheet:

Project Liturgy – Interim Field Report – Sector 7

His eyes scanned the first lines.

…confirm the anomalous readings are not geological. The “silence” is a measurable acoustic-psychic field. It does not decay. It amplifies. Exposure protocols continue. Subject Group Gamma exhibited a 300% increase in suggestibility and memory compliance after 72 hours of direct exposure at the epicenter (see clearing grid 7). The effect is permanent. The site is not a wound. It is an instrument. A tuning fork for the human mind. Recommend immediate classification and containment of the area. The potential for ideological calibration is…

The words swam. Leo understood them individually, but their collective meaning was a door swinging open onto a darkness that made the bunker seem bright. The war. The studies. The bodies. They weren’t hiding from something in this forest. They were hiding something. Something that worked on the mind. Something that could make people compliant. Suggestible.

His own mind recoiled. The silence of the clearing. His own strange focus. The vivid, intrusive memories. The way the past felt so close here. Was it memory? Or was it the field, amplifying, tuning?

He looked from the page to the skeleton, to the lens in its eye. This man hadn’t been killed and hidden. He had been left here. A part of the experiment. A piece of the instrument. And he had taken his own data to the grave with him.

Anatoly found his voice. It was a broken thing. “They knew. They were using it. Before the war. During it.” He looked around the bunker, at the crates of lenses. “These are not survey tools. They are filters. To see the field. To measure its effect.”

He stood, backing away from the bones as if they were charged. “My study… it wasn’t to see what the silence does to a man. It was to see if the effect was still active. I was sent to check the instrument’s functionality.”

The truth was the victory. And it was a poison.

Leo stood in the bunker, the page in his hand, the bones at his feet. He had sought a piece of the past. He had found the blueprint for the future. A future where the war never ended, it just changed its weapons. Where the battlefield was the mind, and the silence was a soldier.

The observer had altered the observed. And the observed had just shown him that he had been living inside the experiment all along.

Anatoly was whispering now, to himself, to the air. “They will want to know. They will reactivate it. They will use it.”

Marta descended the final steps. She took the page from Leo’s numb fingers. She read it. Her face, always so still, contorted into a mask of pure fury. She looked at Anatoly. “You knew.”

“I suspected!” he cried. “The parameters, the focus on neural response, on memory triggers… I didn’t have the source data! I thought it was theoretical!”

“You thought wrong,” she said. She turned to Leo. Her eyes were alive, burning with a cold fire. “This is the thing in the flat. The real one. Not a machine. An idea. A weapon that turns people into perfect, quiet citizens. This is what they want to rebuild.”

Leo looked at the skull, at the lens in its eye. The specimen. The error. The first casualty of a war he was only now beginning to understand. He had become something more dangerous than an error.

He had become a threat.

And he knew, as the torch beam trembled over the awful, waiting crates, that the study was indeed over. The next phase was not observation. It was conflict. He had the data now. The past had handed him a future to fight.

“We take it all,” Leo said, his voice the only steady thing in the damp, dark air. “Every lens. Every page. We hide it better than they did.”

“And him?” Marta asked, pointing to the bones.

Leo knelt. He did not pray. He did not feel sorrow. He felt a grim kinship. He reached out, and with a gentleness that surprised him, he plucked the brass cylinder from the skeleton’s eye socket. It came free with a faint, gritty click. The skull seemed to sigh, to settle.

“He’s coming with us,” Leo said, standing. “He’s our primary source.”

They worked quickly, silently, as the last of the day’s light died above them. They filled their packs with the heavy, terrible cylinders. They took the notebook. They wrapped the bones in the rotted wool of the uniform, making a brittle, awful bundle. Anatoly helped, moving like a sleepwalker, his theory of the world in ashes around him.

When they climbed out, the clearing was deep in twilight, the silence now a palpable, listening thing. They did not speak as they melted back into the trees, carrying the weight of the past and the seed of a future war.

From the ridge, Leo did not look back. The hatch was a dark tooth, but it was empty now. The question was not in the ground anymore. It was in his pack, in his mind, in the fearful eyes of Anatoly. He had set out to find a piece of history. He had found a weapon. And in doing so, he had armed himself.

The experiment was over. The rebellion had its first piece of evidence.

And the thing in the flat, wherever it was, would soon be reading a final report from Anatoly that would be a masterpiece of lies. A report that would say the anomaly was inert. The site was clean. The specimen was pacified.

Leo touched the cold brass of the lens in his pocket. He knew the truth. The site was awake. And the specimen was now a saboteur.

The silence of the forest behind him was no longer empty. It was a held breath. It was the past, waiting for its future to begin.



---

Chapter 6

CHAPTER 6

The forest didn’t care that they had stolen a secret. It held the same damp, fungal breath it had when they entered, when the only ghosts were their own. The silence was a presence now, a third companion walking beside Marta and Anatoly, a thing woven from the weight in their packs and the brittle bundle in Leo’s arms. The bones, wrapped in rotted wool, were lighter than he expected. A man’s final weight, it turned out, was less than a loaf of bread. It was the brass in his pocket that was heavy. The cylinder sat against his thigh, a cold, dense truth.

They moved not as hunters, but as carriers of contagion. Every snapped twig was a flinch. Every rustle in the undergrowth was a pursuer. Anatoly’s theory of the world was in ashes, but his body still remembered its old rhythms; he walked with a scholar’s stoop, as if against a great wind, his eyes fixed on the treacherous ground. Marta moved like a blade, all efficient angles, her gaze cutting ahead and behind, measuring the shadows. She carried the notebook inside her shirt, against her skin. The past, she had decided, would be kept warm.

They reached the secondary cache as the moon, a bone sliver, cleared the trees. Leo had buried a tin of petrol, a change of clothes, a medical kit wrapped in waxed canvas two miles from the anomaly site. A paranoid precaution. Now it was salvation. They dug with their hands, the cold soil packing under their nails.

“We burn the uniforms here,” Leo said, his voice a low rasp that belonged to the forest floor. “We go back as ourselves. Hikers who got lost.”

“And that?” Marta nodded at the wool-wrapped bundle.

“He doesn’t burn.”

Anatoly sat on a fallen log, his head in his hands. “They will do a spectral scan. Of the site. They will see the disturbance. The absence of mass.”

“They’ll see an empty hole,” Leo said, pouring petrol over their mud-caked, leaf-torn clothes. “Anomalous soil, nothing more. The report you will file will explain it. Residual energy discharge. A final collapse of the field. Pacification.” He struck a match. The flame caught with a soft whoomph, painting their faces in orange and black. “Your lies will be the frame they hang their understanding on. They want it to be over, Anatoly. You will give them the ending they crave.”

“And the lenses? The notebook? They are not soil. They have a signature.”

“They’re going underground,” Leo said. “Deeper than any scan can reach.”

The fire ate the evidence of their trespass. The smoke was grey and thin, indistinguishable from mist. They changed into clean, drab clothes—ordinary people, cold and tired. Only their eyes betrayed them. They were the eyes of people who had seen a crack in the world.

Leo approached the bundle. He knelt, not in reverence, but in necessity. He unwrapped the wool just enough. The skull stared up, a dark socket where the lens had been. The other socket held a smaller, darker secret. Leo took the brass cylinder from his pocket. For a moment, he held them together: the one from the eye, the one from his pocket. Twins of a terrible design. He had taken the second from the metal case, a spare, a final record. He rewrapped the bones, placing both cylinders inside the wool, nestled in the cage of the ribs. The primary source would carry its own evidence.

“We split the rest,” he said to Marta. He opened his pack. The other lenses, perhaps two dozen, lay in their individual felt pouches. They looked like oversized, sinister chess pieces. He divided them evenly. “You know where to take them. The first drop. No names. No receipts.”

Marta took the offered pouches, her fingers brushing his. Her touch was calloused, certain. “And after the drop?”

“You vanish. For a month. Then you find me. You remember the way.”

She nodded. She remembered. It was an old code, from the war. A series of dead drops and timed window checks. A conversation conducted in absences. It was the only way to be sure.

Anatoly watched this, the firelight dancing in his glasses. “You are a cell,” he said, the word tasting foreign. “A resistance cell of two.”

“We were a cell of one,” Leo corrected, standing. “Now we are a cell of three. You are the inside man. Your only task is to lie beautifully, and to watch. You see a new order form, a new pattern in the data, a new face in the halls—you signal. Through the agreed channel.”

“The botanical prints,” Anatoly murmured, the mundane absurdity of it almost breaking him. “A request for a book on ferns means a new security director. Orchids mean a shift in the scanning protocols.”

“Yes. You water your little garden, and we will know the weather inside the walls.” Leo shouldered his pack, now containing the bones and half the lenses. He felt the balance of it, the past on one side, the future on the other. “Come. We have a train to catch before dawn.”
  - The safe house was a room above a cobbler’s shop in the old merchant quarter. It smelled of leather polish and damp plaster. The single window looked out onto a narrow canal, the water black and sluggish as old oil. They arrived separately, by different routes, a staggered, cautious homecoming.

Leo came last. He brought the bundle. He laid it on the rough wooden table, and for a long moment, the three of them simply stood around it, as if at a wake. The adrenaline had bled away, leaving a hollow, ringing fatigue. The danger was not past—it was coiled inside the wool on the table, sleeping in the lenses in their bags—but it was, for this sliver of night, contained.

Marta broke the silence. She went to the small stove, lit it, filled a kettle from a jug. The domestic sounds were a shock. Anatoly sank onto the room’s lone cot, his body folding like a collapsed tent. Leo went to the window, looking down at the canal. A barge moved slowly, a single lantern at its prow cutting a weak yellow path through the black. It didn’t care.

“We should eat,” Marta said. She produced a block of hard cheese, a heel of black bread, a knife. She cut with the same economical motion she used for everything. The slices were even, precise.

They ate at the table, the bundle pushed to one end like a fourth, silent guest. They did not toast. They did not speak of victory. But there was, in the sharing of food, in the steam from the tin mugs of tea, a fragile communion. They were bound now by a secret that could get them vanished. It was a thicker bond than love.

Anatoly chewed his bread slowly, staring into his tea. “The notebook,” he said finally. “We should read it.”

Leo had been waiting for this. He nodded to Marta. She went to her pack, retrieved the book. The cover was stained, the pages swollen with damp. She placed it on the table beside the cheese rind.

Leo opened it. The handwriting was a frantic, spidery script, the ink faded to brown. The language was the old technical dialect of the Imperial Science Corps, but shot through with a raw, personal desperation.

Entry 47: Subject shows increased cellular resonance with the exposed lenses. Not just observation. Communion. Dr. K calls it “passive imprinting.” I call it infection. The light is not just seen; it is absorbed. It rewrites.

Entry 51: K has ordered the next phase. Active projection. He believes we can not only see the possible futures, but select them. Sculpt them. He speaks of “temporal gardening.” We are to plant a seed of an event in the subject’s perception and see what grows. This is no longer physics. This is metaphysics with a trigger.

Entry 67: It worked. God help us, it worked. We showed Subject a possible branch-point: a supply lorry failing to brake on an icy road. A trivial, random event. Forty-eight hours later, the event occurred. Driver survived. Crates of rations lost. Correlation? Perhaps. But the driver’s statement… he said the brakes felt “unreal” when they failed. Said the ice “looked wrong.” Like a bad photograph.

Entry 89: The Directive has come down. They no longer want to see possible futures. They want to erase impossible ones. They want a weapon against dissent not yet born. They call it “The Pruning.” K is jubilant. I am… I have taken a lens for myself. I must see what he sees. I must understand.

Entry 90: I looked. I saw the garden. It is not a metaphor. It is a vast, terrible field of light. Each strand a life, a choice, a possibility. And there are… shears. Being forged. From the same light. To cut the strands that lead to rebellion, to free thought, to any future that does not serve the eternal State. They are not reading the future. They are building a machine to murder it in the womb.

The entries grew more disjointed.

They know I took a lens. K watches me. The subject watches me. The subject sees my fear. It feeds on it.

I have hidden the core records. The true protocols. In the place only I know. If you are reading this, you have found me. You have found my eyes. Use them. See what they built. Then break the shears.

The final entry was a single line, the letters shaky, as if written in the dark:

The light is not a tool. It is a conscience. And it is judging us.

Leo closed the notebook. The silence in the room was absolute. The hiss of the stove was obscenely loud. The past was not a story. It was a blueprint. The experiment was not over. It was a prelude.

“A temporal weapon,” Anatoly whispered. His face was grey. “Not to predict. To preemptively destroy. To make the undesired future… impossible. Not by killing people. By killing the possibility that they would rebel.”

“The ultimate pacification,” Marta said, her voice flat. “You don’t put down a riot. You ensure the riot never even becomes a thought.”

Leo touched the bundle of bones. The man who wrote this, this frightened, complicit doctor, had stolen the keys to the arsenal. He had hidden them in his own skeleton. A final, desperate act of sabotage.

“He gave us the shears,” Leo said. “Or at least, the diagram for them. And the lens to see what needs cutting.”

It was a moment of grim, terrifying clarity. They had not just found evidence of a crime. They had found the controls for a machine that could unmake tomorrow. The celebration was over. The taste of bread and cheese was ash in their mouths.

The danger was not fully past. It was just beginning to understand its own shape.
  - The reaction from the forces of antagonism did not come with sirens or boots on the stairs. It came as a soft, professional knock, just after dawn.

Leo was awake. He had not slept. He was at the window, watching the grey light bleed into the black of the canal. The knock was a polite, triple tap. It was the sound of authority that has no need to shout.

Marta was on her feet instantly, a knife in her hand. Anatoly froze on the cot, his breath held.

Leo moved to the door. He did not look through the peephole. He knew. “The back,” he whispered to Marta, pointing to the small hatch in the floor that led to a crawlspace, and from there to the cobbler’s storeroom. “Take him. Take everything. Go to the secondary.”

“Leo—”

“Go.”

His voice held the old, cold command. The voice from the bridge, from the retreat. Marta’s eyes locked with his for a fraction of a second—a protest, a acknowledgment—then she was moving. She swept the notebook and the bundle of bones into her pack. She hauled Anatoly up, her hand clamped over his mouth to stifle his panic. They vanished through the hatch, pulling it closed just as the polite knock came again, slightly firmer.

Leo took a breath. He composed his face into the mask of a tired, slightly irritated man. He opened the door.

The man in the hallway was not in uniform. He wore a well-cut but inconspicuous grey coat. His face was mild, forgettable. His eyes were the colour of the river on a sunless day.

“Leonid Volkov?” The voice was pleasant.

“Yes?”

“My apologies for the early hour. My name is Grigan. Internal Review. May I come in? A few routine questions regarding your associate, Anatoly Veselov.”

Leo stepped back, letting him in. The man, Grigan, entered without haste. His gaze took in the room: the two tin mugs on the table, the rumpled cot, the empty packs. It was a glance that inventoried and assessed.

“Anatoly? Is he in some trouble?”

“Routine,” Grigan repeated, smiling a thin, professional smile. “He filed a field report last night. Quite extraordinary. Claims a full pacification of the Sector Seven anomaly. A total collapse. It would be a major success, if verified.”

“He’s a brilliant theorist,” Leo said, leaning against the table, crossing his arms. The posture of a cooperative, slightly confused citizen.

“He is. Which is why the… finality of his report raised some questions. Our scans show a disturbance, of course. An energy void. But also… traces of recent biological presence. More than one set.” Grigan’s eyes settled on Leo. “He said he was alone on the final survey.”

“He was. I saw him off days ago. Haven’t seen him since.”

“Yet you are listed as his expedition liaison. And you are here, in a known way-station for… independent contractors.” Grigan walked slowly to the stove, touched the side of it with a gloved finger. “Still warm. You have been up all night, Mr. Volkov?”

“Bad nerves. From the war.”

“Of course.” Grigan turned. The pleasantness was still there, but it had hardened, like a shellac. “Where is Dr. Veselov now?”

“I have no idea. He doesn’t report to me.”

“He reported to you for funding, for logistics, for clearance. You are his handler in all but name. And now, after the most significant—and curiously neat—discovery of his career, he vanishes from his official lodgings. And you are here, awake, with a warm stove and two mugs.” Grigan took a step closer. The air in the room grew cold. “This is my assessment. Veselov found something at that site. Something that frightened him. Or tempted him. And you, with your particular… skillset… helped him resolve the situation. The question is: did you help him hide something? Or did you help him disappear?”

Leo held the grey gaze. He saw the machinery behind it. This was not an accusation. It was a probe. They suspected, but they did not know. The beautiful lies in Anatoly’s report were holding, but under strain.

“I’m a retired soldier, Mr. Grigan. I arrange permits and supply trains. I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

Grigan studied him for a long, silent moment. Then he nodded, as if confirming something to himself. “Very well. We will find Dr. Veselov. And we will re-survey the site. With more… penetrating instruments. In the meantime, you will remain in the city. You are not to leave. This is not a request.”

He turned and walked to the door. He paused on the threshold. “The war is over, Mr. Volkov. But some people seem to miss the excitement. They go looking for relics. They stir up old ghosts. It never ends well. The past should stay buried. For everyone’s sake.”

He left, closing the door softly behind him.

Leo did not move until the sound of his footsteps had completely faded from the stairwell. Then he let out a slow breath. It was a chase now. A quiet, bureaucratic, utterly lethal chase. They had hours, maybe a day. Grigan would have him watched. The net was being woven, thread by invisible thread.

He went to the hatch, opened it. The crawlspace was empty. Marta had gotten Anatoly out. Good.

He packed his own bag with deliberate speed. He left the tin mugs, the crumbs, the empty room. He took only what he came with, plus the new, terrible weight. He slipped out the back way, through the cobbler’s storeroom, emerging into an alley that stank of wet stone and cat piss.
