He sat by the cold stove as the full morning light filled the room. The past was not a country. It was not a lock. It was a weapon. Anatol had just placed it in his hands. The question was no longer what was on the other side of the door.

The question was which of them he would turn it on first.

Down on the embankment, the man finished mending his net. He gathered it into his arms, a grey, tangled heap. He did not look at the river. He turned and walked away, his figure growing smaller and smaller until he was absorbed by the city’s vast, grey body. Leo watched him until he was gone. Then he looked at the three objects on his desk, and he began, very carefully, to make a plan of his own.



---

Chapter 17

CHAPTER 17

The plan was not a list. It was not a sequence of moves on a board. It was a chemical reaction, waiting for a catalyst. Leo understood the principle: you assembled the volatile elements, you contained them in a vessel of your own choosing, and then you introduced the spark. The trick was to ensure the resulting fire consumed the laboratory, not the chemist.

He left the flat at dusk. The air had turned sharp, carrying the damp, mineral smell of the river up into the streets. He wore his old coat, the one that held the shape of his shoulders and nothing else. In the pocket, the cigarette case was a cold, flat weight. The wire and the portfolio remained on the desk, in plain view. They were part of the vessel. They were the bait in the trap, but the trap was not for the mouse. It was for the man who set the mouse to running.

He walked without apparent purpose, a man taking the evening air. His route was a slow, deliberate arc away from the river, through districts where the buildings leaned together like tired old men sharing a secret. The scars of the war here were not grand, bombed-out facades; they were subtler. A missing iron railing, melted down for shells. Patches of newer, brighter brick where a mortar had found a home. Windows still boarded over, not because the glass was gone, but because the people were. The city was a body healing around shrapnel it could not expel.

He stopped at a public telephone kiosk on a deserted corner. The glass was webbed with a single, frost-like crack. He stepped inside, the smell of stale urine and wet metal closing around him. He did not dial the number for the exchange, the one that would connect him to Ferris’s bland, efficient voice. That line was a tripwire. He dialed another, a number he had pulled from the static of a dead man’s file, a number that belonged to a minor functionary in the Bureau of Sanitary Engineering. A man named Karel, who had a wife with expensive tastes and a ledger book that did not survive the last audit.

The phone rang four times before it was answered. A woman’s voice, clipped and wary. “Yes?”

“I need to speak with Karel. Regarding the drainage inspection on Kherov Street.” Leo’s voice was flat, bureaucratic. It was the voice of a man delivering bad news about pipes.

A pause. A muffled hand over the receiver. Then a man came on, his breath slightly ragged. “This is he. There is no scheduled inspection.”

“The schedule has changed,” Leo said. He watched a cat pick its way along the gutter outside the kiosk. “The obstruction is not in the pipe. It is in the ledger. Room 407. One hour.”

He hung up before Karel could form a question. Questions were for people who still believed in answers. Leo stepped out of the kiosk. The catalyst was introduced.

Room 407 was not in the Bureau of Sanitary Engineering. It was in the old Metropol Hotel, a grand, soot-stained edifice that had been converted into ministerial offices. The fourth floor housed low-priority archives—water rights disputes, pre-war property surveys, the geological assessments for the now-abandoned subway expansion. It was a place of silent, papery decay. The kind of place where a man like Karel, sweating through his shirt, would go to make a problem disappear. The kind of place Anatol had chosen because it was a perfect, plausible non-entity. A lockbox for ghosts.

Leo took a longer, more circular route. He watched the reflections in shop windows, the patterns of the few other pedestrians. He saw no obvious shadows. They wouldn’t be obvious. They would be the man reading a newspaper on a bench, the woman adjusting her shoe. They would be the ones who did not look at him at all. He was counting on it.

The Metropol’s grand lobby was a cavern of marble and diminished ambition. A single clerk dozed behind the reception desk. The elevator cage stood open, a relic of another time. Leo took the stairs, his footsteps echoing in the hollow, dusty well. The fourth-floor corridor was long and dim, lit by sconces with half the bulbs dead. The numbers on the doors were brass, tarnished to a dull green. 401. 403. 405.

The door to 407 was unremarkable. Dark wood, a simple knob. He did not take the key from his pocket yet. He stood before it, listening. The building sighed around him, the sound of old pipes and settling stone. From behind other doors, the faint, dry rustle of paper. Nothing else.

He took out the cigarette case. Opened it. The skeleton key lay there, innocent as a fallen tooth. He fitted it into the lock. It turned with a smooth, well-oiled click. Anatol had maintained his locks, if nothing else.

Leo pushed the door open.

The room was not large. A single window, grimy with city filth, looked out onto a light well. The walls were lined with wooden filing cabinets, their drawers labeled in a neat, faded hand. In the center of the room stood a plain table and a single wooden chair. On the table was a cardboard file box. And on top of the box, weighing down a sheaf of papers, was a river stone, smooth and grey.

He closed the door behind him. The lock engaged with a solid, final sound. He did not approach the table immediately. He went to the filing cabinets. He pulled a drawer at random. It slid open smoothly. Inside were folders, each tabbed with a date and a case number. He lifted one out. Kherov Street, Parcel 12-B, Dispute: Water Access vs. Right-of-Way. 1937. He opened it. The documents were real, filled with the dense, tedious language of petty officialdom. He tried another drawer. Geological Survey, Sector G-9, Substrata Stability. 1939.

The room was real. Its purpose was real. Its boring, profound reality was its perfect camouflage. This was Anatol’s genius. He had not hidden the past in a secret vault. He had buried it in plain sight, in the most forgettable cemetery in the city.

Leo turned to the box on the table. He moved the river stone. It was cool, heavy in his hand. The weight of indifference. He set it aside.

The top sheet was a carbon copy of a transfer manifest. From: Central Evidence Repository, Annex 7. To: Metropol Archives, Room 407. For: Long-term storage, Category Delta (Non-Judicial). The date was six weeks after Anya’s disappearance. The listed items were vague, meaningless. Personal effects (1 lot). Correspondence (1 bundle). Miscellaneous papers. The authorizing signature was a scrawl he did not recognize. The receiving signature was Anatol’s, neat and precise.

Beneath the manifest, the box was not full. It contained perhaps two inches of material. Leo lifted the first item out.

It was a sketchbook. A simple, cloth-bound artist’s sketchbook, the kind sold in every stationer’s before the war. The cover was smudged with charcoal. He opened it.

The first pages were studies. A bowl of pears, rendered with a confident, searching line. The view from a window—his window, the river a broad, grey stroke. A man’s hand resting on a table. His hand. He remembered the afternoon she had drawn it, how she had complained about his fidgeting. “Be a rock, Leo. Just for five minutes.” He had asked what she saw. She had smiled without looking up. “I see a history of holding on. And of letting go.”

He turned the page. The drawings became quicker, more urgent. Soldiers in a café, their faces hollow with exhaustion. The geometry of broken roof beams. A child staring at a tank. The war, not as a event, but as a series of stolen moments, of human shapes pressed into unnatural postures by an invisible, terrible weight.

Then, near the middle of the book, the red lines began.

They were not illustrations. They were diagrams, but not of machines. They were flowcharts of influence. Lines connecting names to ministries, ministries to procurement orders, orders to warehouse numbers. Petrov to Military Supply Directorate (Krasnov). Krasnov to Sub-contractor (Varga Holdings). Varga to Shipping Lane (Black Sea route 3). The connections were in black ink. The lines tracing the flow of materials—copper, rubber, morphine—were drawn in a vivid, waxy red pencil. Her red pencil.

She had not just been drawing the city’s wounds. She had been dissecting its circulatory system, finding the points where the lifeblood of the war effort was being siphoned away. It was meticulous, forensic. And on the page opposite one particularly dense web, she had written a single line, not in her usual script, but in block capitals, as if to carve the words into the paper:

THEY ARE NOT STEALING FROM THE WAR. THE WAR IS THE COVER. THEY ARE STEALING FROM THE FUTURE.

Leo’s breath felt thin in his chest. He had known she was asking questions. He had warned her. “These are not corners you shine a light into, Anya. The things that live there bite.” She had kissed his forehead. “Then I’ll draw their teeth, my love. Every single one.”

He set the sketchbook down, his fingers leaving damp prints on the cover. The next item was a bundle of letters, tied with a faded ribbon. They were his. Every letter he had written to her during his first deployment, when the war was still new and they both believed in certain endings. His own words, in his own hand, filled with a longing that now felt like the artifact of a different species of man. “When I return, we will go to the coast. Just the sound of the sea. No bells, no whistles.” She had kept them. She had kept them and then she had hidden them here, with evidence that could get her killed. Why? A reminder of what she was fighting for? Or a reminder of what she was risking?

Beneath the letters was a slim, leather-bound notebook. Anya’s personal journal. He opened it to a random page.

October 12. Met with L. again at the Green Lamp. He is terrified. Says the “harvest” from the eastern front is more than anyone authorized. Not just spoils. People. Skilled people. Engineers, chemists. A “brain trust” being funneled west, off the books. He gave me a name. Vorst. I have never heard it. Leo is home on leave. He is so quiet. He sees the shadows under my eyes. He thinks it’s the work. It is. But not the work he knows. I cannot tell him. It would be a hook in his mouth, and they would reel him in. My love, my anchor. I have to keep you in the dark to keep you safe. It is the worst kind of betrayal.

Leo closed his eyes. The silence of the room was absolute. He could hear the blood moving in his own ears. A hook in his mouth. She had seen it, years before it happened. She had kept him blind to protect him, and in doing so, had made him the perfect instrument for them later. A man with no history, with a void where his grief should be. A blank slate they could write their lies upon.

The final item in the box was a single sheet of heavy paper. It was a charcoal portrait. Of him. He was looking slightly away from the viewer, his profile sharp against a dark, undefined background. She had captured the set of his jaw, the tension in the neck, the watchfulness in the eye. It was not a portrait of a lover. It was a portrait of a sentinel. A man standing on a wall, waiting for an enemy he could not yet see. At the bottom, in her small, precise script, she had written: Leo. The fault line.

She had seen that, too.

He stood over the table, the artifacts of his own life and her death laid out like evidence in a case he was only now beginning to understand. The past was not a country. It was a weapon. Anatol had said it, and now Leo held the cold, fitted steel of it in his hands. Anya had not been erased by slow, indifferent forces. She had been targeted. She had drawn a red line around a conspiracy so vast it used the war as its backdrop, and for that, she had been disappeared. Not by the enemy. By the men on her own side. The men who were stealing the future.

And they had used him to clean up the last loose end: her memory. They had made him her ghost, a benchmark to measure new lies against. The fury that rose in him was not hot. It was glacial, a vast, slow-moving ice that promised to grind everything in its path to dust.

A sound in the corridor. A soft scuff of a shoe.

Leo did not look at the door. He carefully, methodically, placed the items back into the box. The journal, the letters, the sketchbook. He left the portrait on top. He replaced the river stone, anchoring the past once more. He stepped away from the table, moving to the side of the room, into the deeper shadow between two filing cabinets.

The lock turned. The door opened.

Karel stood in the doorway, a thin man with a sheen of sweat on his pale forehead. He wore a civil servant’s suit, too large for his frame. His eyes darted around the room, skipping over the shadows where Leo stood, and landed on the box on the table.

“Hello?” Karel’s voice was a dry whisper. “Is someone there?”

Leo said nothing. He watched.

Karel took a hesitant step into the room, then another. He was drawn to the table like a moth to a flame. He saw the box. He saw the portrait on top. He leaned closer, peering at the drawing. His breath hitched. He recognized the face.

“Oh, god,” he breathed. He reached out a trembling hand, not for the portrait, but for the river stone. He moved it aside. He lifted the portrait. He saw the sketchbook beneath. He did not open it. He didn’t need to. He knew what it was. His shoulders slumped, as if the weight of the entire room had settled on them.

“It’s real,” he murmured to himself. “She really did… and he has it. He knows.”

He. Not Leo. The man Karel was afraid of. The man who had sent him.

Karel’s head snapped up. He seemed to remember where he was. He fumbled in his jacket pocket and pulled out a small, cloth-wrapped bundle. He unfolded it on the table next to the box. It was a set of lockpicks, fine and professional. He was not here to retrieve the box. He was here to plant something.

But he froze, the picks glittering in the weak light. He was staring at the portrait again, at the face of the fault line. His own fear was a mirror, and in it, Leo saw the reflection of the trap about to spring. Karel was the mouse. And the man who sent him was watching, waiting for the mouse to trigger the mechanism.

Leo stepped out of the shadows.

Karel gasped, stumbling back against the table. The lockpicks scattered, chiming on the floor. “You! You’re… you’re supposed to be…”

“Dead?” Leo finished, his voice quiet. “Or on my way here, so you could finish your job and be gone before I arrived?” He took a step forward. “Who sent you, Karel? Who told you to come to Room 407 and plant evidence in that box? Evidence that would link me to Anya’s ‘treason’? To close her file for good, and mine with it?”

Karel’s mouth worked soundlessly. His eyes were wide, white-rimmed with terror. It was not the terror of a man caught in a crime. It was the terror of a man caught between two forces, either of which could crush him. “I don’t know… a message… a threat to my family…”

“A name,” Leo said, taking another step. The space between them was charged, brittle.

“Vorst!” Karel blurted, the word a desperate exhalation. “The message came from someone called Vorst!”

The name from Anya’s journal. A name that moved in the deepest shadows, that harvested brains and stole futures.

Leo nodded slowly. “And you were to plant what, exactly?”

Karel pointed a shaking finger at the scattered picks. “With those. To make it look like you… like you had broken in. That you were her accomplice. That you were silencing the last trace of her.” He was crying now, thin, helpless tears. “They said if I did it, my ledger would be lost. My wife would be safe. Please. I have children.”

Leo looked at the man, this pared-down, useless core of a human being. He was not an enemy. He was a symptom. He was the kind of small, necessary failure the system produced and then used for its patient corrections.

“Pick them up,” Leo said.

Karel stared, uncomprehending.

“The lockpicks. Pick them up. Put them back in your pocket.”

With fumbling, clumsy hands, Karel obeyed. He gathered the fine tools, wrapped them, stuffed them into his jacket.

“Now,” Leo said, his voice low and utterly calm. “You will go home to your wife and your children. You will tell Vorst, or whoever contacts you, that you succeeded. That the evidence is planted. That Leo is now irrevocably tied to Anya’s crime.”

“But… it’s not.”

“It doesn’t matter. You will say it is. Your safety depends on their belief that I am now the painted target. Do you understand?”

Karel nodded, a frantic bob of his head.

“If you deviate,” Leo continued, “if you warn them, if you run, they will kill your family. And then I will find you. Not to kill you. To show you what is in this box. To show you exactly what your cowardice has been serving. Go.”

Karel did not need to be told twice. He fled, leaving the door swinging open behind him. His footsteps pattered away down the corridor, fading into the building’s sigh.

Leo walked to the door. He closed it. He locked it with the skeleton key. He returned to the table. He looked at the portrait of the fault line. He looked at the box containing the weapon of his past.

The catalyst had worked. The reaction had begun. Karel was the spark, and he would now carry the false fire back to his masters. They would believe their trap was set, that Leo was now neatly framed, a problem ready to be solved. They would look at the vessel he had built—the wire, the portfolio, the desperate, predictable move to Room 407—and they would see exactly what they expected to see: a man playing a game he was destined to lose.

They would not see the fault line opening beneath their feet.

He took the portrait. He folded it once, carefully, along a line that did not intersect his face, and slipped it inside his coat. He left the rest. The box, the stone, the sketchbook with its red lines—they were part of the vessel now, too. Let them find it. Let them see how much he knows. Let them rush to contain the damage they thought was contained.

He turned off the light and left the room, locking the door for the last time. The corridor was empty. The city’s vast, grey body waited outside. He descended the stairs, a man with a plan that was no longer a chemical equation. It was a seismic event. He had the past in his pocket, a portrait of a man he used to be. He had the future on a wire, a name—Vorst—that was a key to a different kind of lock.

He stepped out into the night. The air was colder now. Down by the river, the lights of the embankment were smudged yellow halos in the fog. He began to walk, not toward his flat, but toward the river’s edge. He needed to see the water, the grey, muscular, indifferent flow of it.

He reached the stone wall of the embankment. The water moved below, dark and powerful, carrying its secrets to the sea. A figure was there, a silhouette against the glow of the city on the opposite bank. A man, sitting on a bench, facing the water.

As Leo approached, the man did not turn. But his posture, the set of his shoulders, was familiar in a way that sent a cold, electric current down Leo’s spine. It was the man with the net. But his net was gone. His hands were empty, resting on his knees.

Leo stopped a few paces away. The man spoke, his voice gravelly and calm, carried on the damp air.

“She always liked this spot,” the man said. “Said the river was the only honest thing left in the city. It takes everything, and asks for nothing.”

Leo’s hand was inside his coat, on the folded portrait. “You knew her.”

“I mended her nets,” the man said. He finally turned his head. His face was worn, leathered by wind and water, his eyes the color of the river under a winter sky. “Not fishing nets. The nets she cast into the deep, dark water. To catch the big, ugly things that fed on the bottom.”

“Who are you?”

“A fisherman. A mender. A man who knows that some breaks can’t be fixed, only made strong enough to hold a different kind of catch.” He looked back at the water. “She said you were the anchor. The one thing that couldn’t be moved. She was wrong, of course. Everyone can be moved. The question is what direction you choose to drag.”

“Vorst,” Leo said.

The fisherman was silent for a long moment. “A current,” he said finally. “Not a man. A force. It pulls things into the deep. It pulled her. It’s pulling you. You have the key. You’ve seen the room. Do you feel the current yet, Leo?”

Leo did. It was the pull of the past, swift and deep and deadly. “What do you want?”

“To see what an anchor does when it’s not holding something in place,” the fisherman said, rising to his feet. He was taller than he’d seemed from the window. “When it’s dropped into the current itself.” He began to walk away, along the embankment, his figure blending into the night fog.

“Wait,” Leo said.

The fisherman did not stop or turn. His voice floated back, already half-absorbed by the river’s murmur. “Room 407 was the mirror. The river is the window. Look into it. You’ll see what’s coming.”

And then he was gone, swallowed by the city’s vast, grey body.

Leo stood alone. He looked down at the black water. It did not care about wars, or conspiracies, or love, or loss. It moved, relentless, toward the sea. He saw his own reflection, fractured and shifting on its surface. And for a moment, just a moment, he saw another face superimposed on his own—not Anya’s, but a stranger’s, cold and austere, looking back at him from the depths. A face that knew him. A face that was waiting.

The current had him. He was no longer deciding when the ground would give way.

He was learning how to swim in the collapse.



---

Chapter 18

CHAPTER 18

The flat was a cage. The silence after the fisherman’s departure was not an absence of sound, but a presence. It was the hum of the current he’d spoken of, a vibration in the marrow of the building, in the stone of the sill beneath Leo’s palms. He stood at the window long after the fog had erased the last suggestion of a figure on the embankment. The man with the net was gone. The river was a sheet of hammered lead, featureless and cold.

Room 407 was the mirror. The river is the window.

He had looked into the water and seen a stranger’s face. Not a ghost from the war, not the remembered lines of Anya’s smile or the grim set of Vorst’s jaw in a photograph. A new face. A future face. It was the logic of the past future, a trap he had not understood until now. You dig into what was, expecting to find the roots of what is. But the past is not a root cellar; it is a quarry. And sometimes, you don’t dig up bones. You dig up a seam of ore that runs forward, into a time you haven’t lived yet, waiting to be smelted and forged into a chain.

The key was in his pocket. A simple, cold weight. He had taken it from the false-bottomed drawer in Room 407, from the space that held nothing but dust and the scent of her perfume, faded to a ghost of almonds and regret. He had thought it was a key to a lock. A door. A box. A secret. He had been a child playing with a artillery shell, thinking it was a interesting rock.

He left the flat. The stairs groaned under his weight, the same complaint they had made for fifty years. The sound was a part of the building’s truth, more reliable than any memory he possessed. Out on the street, the city was performing its evening pantomime. Lights winked on in windows, not with warmth, but with the grim determination of people defying the encroaching dark. A tram clattered past, sparks snapping from its overhead wire like tiny, dying stars. Leo walked. He had no destination. Movement was the opposite of being an anchor; it was a test of the fisherman’s thesis.

He walked away from the river, into the labyrinth of narrow streets where the buildings leaned together like conspirators. The victory he had felt after Room 407—the brittle, triumphant clarity of having found the last piece of her trail—was ash in his mouth. It had been a false summit. He had climbed a hill only to see the true mountain range ahead, black and jagged against the horizon of his ignorance. Anya had not left him a trail to follow. She had left him a diagnosis. And the disease was progressive.

He found himself outside the closed façade of the Orpheum Cinema. The marquee was dark, the posters for last year’s films bleached and torn, flapping like ragged skin. She had loved this place. They had sat in the back row, her hand in his, watching American musicals that painted a world of impossible color and resolution. The stories were simple. The problems were solved by a song. He remembered the feel of her shoulder against his, the way she would hum the melodies days later, a private, persistent joy. He had believed, then, in the possibility of a life that was not a series of strategic retreats. He had been a fool.

The alley beside the cinema was a gully of shadows. He entered it, not as a man seeking a shortcut, but as a diver testing the pressure of the deep. The brick walls were slick with damp. At the rear, a metal door, once a fire exit, was sealed with a rusted hasp and a padlock. He stopped. He did not need to try the key. He knew. The shape of the lock, the particular corrosion around the keyhole—it was a match. This was not a discovery. It was a confirmation. The key was not for a room in a faceless hotel. It was for a forgotten door in a place that belonged to her, to them, to the past they had built together before the war scraped it all away.

He fitted the key into the padlock. It turned with a gritty, oil-less smoothness that was obscene. The hasp fell open with a clank that was too loud in the confined space. He pushed the door. It swung inward on complaining hinges, revealing not the cinema’s backstage, but a void. A staircase led down, into a darkness that smelled of earth and stale electricity.

He descended.

The steps were concrete, worn in the centers. A single, bare bulb hung at the bottom, unlit. He found a switch on the wall. He flicked it. Nothing. He moved by touch, one hand on the cold, sweating wall. The air grew colder, damper. He counted twenty steps. Then the wall fell away, and his outstretched hand met nothing.

He stood in a space that breathed. A low, subterranean hum vibrated through the soles of his boots. He fumbled for his lighter, the cheap steel one he’d carried since the war. He sparked it. The flame was a tiny, desperate sun, pushing back the black for a radius of a few feet.

He was in a cellar, but not for storage. The room was long and narrow, a concrete tube. Along one wall ran a workbench, cluttered not with tools, but with instruments. He recognized a seismograph’s drum, a galvanometer, a bank of vacuum tubes in a wooden housing, their glass grey with dust. Wires snaked across the floor, bundled and taped, leading to a central apparatus on a metal table. It was a machine of some kind, all copper coils and iron cores, with a large, circular face like a ship’s porthole made of smoked glass. It was dead. Silent.

This was not Anya’s. She was a historian, an archivist of human folly. This was a laboratory. A cold, precise place for measuring things that could not be seen.

He moved the lighter’s flame along the bench. Papers were neatly stacked under a metal ruler. Diagrams. Schematics. Equations in a tight, angular hand that was not Anya’s. He leaned closer. The symbols were not just mathematical; they were topological, maps of folded spaces, of temporal gradients. At the top of one sheet was a letterhead, embossed and faded: The Vorst Institute for Applied Metaphysics.

The name was a cold finger on his spine. Vorst. The current.

Beneath the schematics was a journal. A simple, black leather notebook. He opened it. Anya’s handwriting flooded the page, but it was not her usual flowing script. It was frantic, compressed, the letters biting into the paper.

He is not a man. He is a process. A function. The Institute sought to map the inertia of history, to find the points of applied leverage. They called it “Temporal Mechanics.” A joke. There is no mechanics. Only will. Vorst is the will of the past to perpetuate itself. It identifies nodes—people, places, events—and applies pressure. It is a river cutting a canyon. The canyon is the future.

I am a node. Leo is a node. Our love was a confluence. He saw it. Vorst saw it. He wants the confluence. He wants to redirect the flow.

The machine is not a viewer. It is a plow. It doesn’t observe time. It furrows it. It creates a channel. He tried it. The test runs were… audible. A sound from the other side of a wall. A scratching. Then a voice. My voice. Giving instructions. From where? From when?

I have taken the key. I have hidden the core component. He cannot complete the calibration without it. He will look for me. He will use Leo to find me. The anchor. He must not be moved. I must make Leo hate me. I must become a break he cannot mend. It is the only way to slacken the line. To make the anchor drag, useless, along the bottom.

God, let him hate me. Let him believe I left him for the money, for the safety, for another man. Let him believe I am shallow and cowardly and dead. It is the only kindness I have left to give him.

The words blurred. The lighter’s flame licked his thumb and he hissed, dropping it. The darkness rushed back in, absolute, a physical weight. He crouched on the cold floor, the journal clutched to his chest. The hum in the room was not in the walls. It was in his head. The collapse was not around him. It was inside.

Every moment of the past five years rearranged itself. Her distance in those final months before she vanished. The cruel, casual words she’d engineered. The staged discovery of the travel documents for a neutral country, the large withdrawal from their shared account. The hint, dropped to a mutual friend, of a wealthy patron waiting across the border. He had consumed it all, a poison he believed was the truth. He had let the bitterness harden in him, a carapace to protect the raw, screaming thing beneath. He had cherished his hatred. It was the only thing he had left of her.

It was the gift she had intended.

He had not been hunting her. He had been performing a role in her design. His grief, his obsession, his dogged pursuit—all of it was a piece of her strategy. He was a counter-weight, a dragged anchor, meant to create a drag in Vorst’s current. He was not the protagonist of his own story. He was a instrument, played by a woman who loved him enough to make him despise her.

The false victory of Room 407 shattered completely. He had not found her. He had found her instructions. And he had arrived too late to follow them.

A sound.

Not from the journal. Not from his own choked breath.

From the machine.

A low, power-surge thrum, like a giant transformer waking. A faint, blue light flickered behind the smoked glass porthole. It pulsed, arrhythmic, sickly. The vacuum tubes on the bench began to glow, a dim orange at their filaments, growing brighter. The hum escalated, climbing in pitch and volume, filling the concrete tube until it vibrated in his teeth.

He scrambled back, pressing against the workbench. The machine was active. Remotely. Or on a timer. Or it was reacting to his presence, to the journal, to the key.

The light behind the glass swirled, coalescing. It was not a image, not quite. It was a pattern, a interference of shadows and cold light. It resolved into a shape that was almost a face. It was the face from the river. Austere. Sexless. Ageless. Eyes that were not eyes, but depths.

A voice issued from a grille below the porthole. It was not a human voice. It was the sound of the hum given words, a vibration shaped into phonemes. It was dry, papery, infinite.

“Leo.”

He could not speak. His throat was sealed.

“You have the component. You have the key. You are in the calibration chamber.” The voice held no emotion. It was a statement of facts. “The node is responsive. The confluence is still potent. This is efficient.”

“Where is she?” The words were torn from him, raw.

“Anya Markov is offline. Her utility was in the creation of the anchor. You are the anchor. Your fixation is the engine. Your presence here completes the circuit.”

The blue light pulsed, and for an instant, the face clarified. He saw a man, but a man like a statue eroded by wind, all sharp planes and hollows. Vorst.

“You made her leave. You made her make me hate her.”

“We applied pressure. She made a choice. A sacrificial play. It demonstrated the depth of the confluence. It made you stronger. More focused. A purer instrument.” The light flickered. The face seemed to look around the cellar. “You have seen the journal. You understand the theory. History has mass. It has currents. This device does not predict. It imprints. It takes a possible future—a likely future, given the mass of the past—and gives it inertia. It makes it sticky. It is the difference between a riverbank of loose soil and one of poured concrete. The water’s path becomes inevitable.”

“What future?” Leo whispered.

“Order,” the voice said, and the word was a tomb closing. “The war was a chaos. A fever. The world is weak, sick, stumbling. It requires a firm hand. A single, guiding will, informed by the full weight of what has been, to dictate what must be. My will. The future is one of seamless, silent control. No more retreats. No more small, desperate lives mending nets. A world that is a finished thing. A perfect, sterile monument.”

Leo saw it. A world without the grit of chance. Without the messy, defiant love of an Anya. Without the bitter, beautiful struggle of a man on a riverbank. A world of grey, smooth surfaces. A world that had already happened, forever.

“She stole something. A core component.”

“A damping rod. A piece of null-alloy. Without it, the imprint is… crude. It creates turbulence. Collateral fractures in the timeline. She thought she could stop me. She only made the process more destructive. The future will still come. But it will be born screaming, in a room of broken glass. You have the rod, Leo. It is the key you carry. It is not for a lock. It is for this machine.”

He looked at the key in his hand, the cold, simple metal. It was a part of this monstrous thing. Anya had not hidden it to keep it from Vorst. She had hidden it to keep it from him. To keep him from being used to complete the machine. She had hoped he would never find this place.

“You want me to give it to you.”

“I want you to install it. You are the anchor. Your hand on the rod, at the moment of calibration, will tie the imprinted future to your node. To your pain. To your love for her. It will give it a heart. A terrible, beating heart. It will make it real.”

The audacity of it was cosmic. Vorst was not just building a machine. He was building a myth. And he needed Leo’s soul as the foundation stone.

“And if I refuse? If I break it?”

The hum deepened. The blue light flickered, and for a second, the face vanished, replaced by a rapid, terrifying flicker of images on the glass: the river in flood, the cinema collapsing in fire, a thousand faces—strangers—contorted in silent screams, melting into one another. The collateral fractures.

“Then the future is still born. But it is a monster. And the first thing it consumes is every point of light that once shone in the darkness of the past. Her memory. Your pain. The few good days you had. It will all be sanded away, leaving only the smooth, featureless narrative of control. With the rod, the transition is… surgical. Clean. A mercy, by comparison.”

It was the devil’s bargain, offered not for his soul, but for his grief. He could let the world become a silent, efficient hell, or he could let it become a screaming, chaotic one that erased the very reason he had to fight.

The fisherman’s words echoed. To see what an anchor does when it’s dropped into the current itself.

He was in the current. It was pulling him toward the machine. He looked at the key, the damping rod. He looked at the journal in his other hand, at her desperate, loving script.

He had spent years learning how to swim in the collapse of his own life. Now he had to swim in the collapse of time.

He stood up. The hum was a physical force, pressing on him. He walked toward the machine. The blue light bathed him, cold and antiseptic. He saw his reflection in the smoked glass, superimposed over the austere, waiting face of Vorst.

“You believe you are giving me a choice,” Leo said, his voice steady now, a flat stone in the torrent. “You are a function. A process. You only understand pressure and nodes. You don’t understand what an anchor does.”

He raised the key. He did not move to the machine’s access panel. He turned and, with all the strength of his betrayal, his love, his honed and useless core, he drove the key into the journal. He speared Anya’s words, her sacrifice, her final, terrible kindness. He pinned the book to the wooden workbench.

The hum stuttered. The light flared.

“What are you doing?” The voice was still devoid of emotion, but there was a new density to it, a confusion.

“An anchor doesn’t just hold,” Leo said, stepping back from the bench, from the machine, from the offered bargain. “It also rips free. It drags. It scours the bottom. You wanted my pain to power your future? Take it. It’s right there. It’s nailed down. But you don’t get me.”

He turned and ran for the stairs. The hum became a shriek. The blue light flashed blindingly, strobing, freezing his flight in jagged snapshots. The sound of tearing metal, of shattering glass, of a fundamental wrongness screamed in frequencies that hurt his teeth. He did not look back.

He burst out into the alley, the cold night air a slap. He slammed the metal door shut behind him. From within, through the iron, he heard not an explosion, but an unwinding—a vast, metallic sigh, followed by a profound, echoing silence that was worse than any noise.

He leaned against the brick wall, gasping. He had not saved the world. He had not beaten Vorst. He had not found Anya.

He had taken the anchor of his own life and driven it into the heart of the machine. He had offered his past as a sacrifice, not to a god, but to a malfunction. He had chosen the screaming, fractured future over the silent, polished one. He had chosen to preserve the chaos, the pain, the love, the memory of her hand in his in a dark cinema. He had chosen a world where mending a net was still possible, even if it was only a series of small, necessary failures.

He walked away from the Orpheum. The city’s lights seemed different. Softer. More fragile. The current still pulled. Vorst was still out there, a force, a process. The machine was damaged, not destroyed. The future was still coming.

But it would not have his heart. It would only have his ghost, nailed to a workbench in a hole in the ground.

He reached the embankment. The river was there, grey, muscular, indifferent. He looked into the black water. His reflection was alone. The stranger’s face was gone. He saw only Leo, weary to his bones, his own history now a weapon he had spent.

He had learned how to swim in the collapse. Now he had to learn how to build in the ruins of a future that was already breaking, somewhere in the dark, behind a locked door. The fisherman had been right. He was no longer an anchor holding something in place.

He was a drag, moving through deep water, and he had just hooked something vast, and terrible, and awake.



---

Chapter 19

CHAPTER 19

The river didn’t care that he had unmade a future. It moved the same way it had when he was a boy skipping stones, when he was a soldier watching the bridges burn, when he was a ghost watching a man mend a net. Grey, muscular, indifferent. Leo stood on the embankment until the cold from the stones seeped through his soles and climbed his legs. His reflection in the black water was a smudge of weariness. The stranger’s face, the borrowed ghost from the machine’s polished tomorrow, was gone. He was only himself again. The feeling was not a homecoming. It was an amputation.

He turned his back on the water. The city’s lights were softer, yes, but it was the softness of a bruise. A tenderness that promised deeper pain. He walked. He had no destination. His feet carried him away from the river, into the arterial maze of streets where the buildings leaned together like conspirators. The profound, echoing silence from behind the Orpheum’s iron door was inside him now. He had not heard an explosion. He had heard a machine exhale. He had felt a future go slack.

He found himself outside the cinema. The marquee was dark, the posters for the musicals faded and torn. He tried the handle of the side door, the one that led up to the projection booth. It was locked. He stood there, his forehead against the cold wood. He could almost smell the dust and old velvet, almost feel the weight of her shoulder against his in the dark. A memory, now. Just a memory. Not a component in a design. He had taken that from Vorst. He had made it his own again by making it useless.

He walked on.

The flat felt like a set from a play that had closed. The air was stale, tasting of chicory and solitude. He did not turn on the light. He went to the window. The man with the net was gone. The embankment was empty, just the river and the sodium glow of a distant lamp. He saw the cup still on the sill where he’d left it. He picked it up. The dregs of the coffee had formed a thin, cracked skin. He poured it out the window, watched the dark liquid vanish into the greater dark below.

The bell rang.

It was the same thin, tinny sound. It didn’t startle him. It felt inevitable, like the next note in a song he’d forgotten he was singing. He did not move. He watched the empty embankment. The bell rang again, two short, insistent jabs, a punctuation mark to his long, silent sentence.

He walked to the door. His footsteps were loud in the hollow room. He did not ask who it was. He opened the door.

Mikhail stood there. He wasn’t backlit by weak afternoon sun, but by the jaundiced glow of the hallway bulb. He looked pared down. His face was the colour of old paper, his eyes two burnt holes. He held his left arm close to his body, the hand tucked into his coat. He didn’t speak. He just looked at Leo, a long, assessing look that started at his eyes and travelled down to his boots, as if checking for damage, for blood, for proof.

“Come in,” Leo said.

Mikhail stepped over the threshold. He moved stiffly, a man favouring an injury he wouldn’t name. He went to the window, looked out at the same nothing Leo had been watching.

“It’s done?” Mikhail’s voice was rough, scraped raw.

“It’s damaged.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It’s the only one I have.” Leo leaned against the doorframe, the wood solid against his spine. “I gave it my past. All of it. The raw data. I fed the machine a ghost and it… choked on it. There was no explosion. There was an unwinding.”

Mikhail’s shoulders tightened. “An unwinding.”

“A sigh. Then silence.”

“And Vorst?”

“Gone. Before it happened. He knew. He let me do it. He said I was making a choice between two futures. I chose the broken one.”

Mikhail turned from the window. His face was grim. “You chose the one with variables. The one he can’t perfectly predict. The one with us in it.” He finally took his left hand from his coat. It was wrapped in a crude bandage, linen gone brown and stiff with old blood. “He sent men. After you left for the Orpheum. Not his polished ghosts. Men. From here. Thugs with pipes and a willingness to work.”

Leo pushed off the doorframe. “How many?”

“Three. They were waiting in my workshop. They knew the layout.” Mikhail’s smile was a thin, pained cut. “They were not there for conversation. They were there to unmake my hands.”

“You stopped them.”

“I persuaded them.” Mikhail’s eyes were flat. “One will not be using his right eye again. Another will walk with a list. The third… he left in a hurry. They were not professionals. They were pressure. A message. Vorst can work with blunt instruments, too. He was reminding me I am still in the world. A world with pipes and sudden pain.”

Leo went to the cupboard, found a bottle of vodka, half-full. He poured two glasses, handed one to Mikhail. They drank. The liquor was a clean, cold fire.

“He’s adapting,” Leo said. “The machine is damaged, so he reverts to older methods. Simpler tools.”

“He is a process,” Mikhail said, echoing Leo’s own words from what felt like years ago. “He finds the path of least resistance. If the future cannot be polished, he will break the present into more manageable pieces.” He looked at his bandaged hand, flexed the fingers slightly, winced. “He knows where I live. He knows where you live. This…” He gestured around the bare flat. “This is over.”

Leo knew he was right. The flat was a shell. A stage. He had been playing a part here, the man watching the river, waiting for the past to catch up. It had caught up. It had walked in the door with a bloody hand. “Where?”

“The fisherman,” Mikhail said. “The boat. It’s time to be on the water. It’s the one place his patterns can’t grip. Currents change. Tides shift. It’s all variables.”

“He’ll look there.”

“He’ll look everywhere. But a boat is a small, moving point. A flat is a fixed coordinate. I’d rather be a difficult point.”

They finished the vodka. The silence between them was not comfortable, but it was earned. It was the silence of two men who had, separately, faced a version of the same crushing weight and had not quite been flattened.

“We need to find Anya,” Leo said. The name felt different in his mouth now. Not a lost object. A necessary variable.

“Vorst will be using her as a focal point,” Mikhail said. “If the machine is damaged, its predictive power is… frayed. Blurred. He will need a lens to bring things back into focus. A person whose future he has already mapped, whose choices he believes he understands. She will be that lens. He will keep her close. Not to hurt her. To use her as a tuning fork.”

“Where does he keep things close?”

Mikhail was silent for a long moment. He refilled his glass himself, drank. “He is not a man of sentiment. He would not keep her in a home, a flat. He would keep her where the work is. Where the process is.”

“The machine was under the Orpheum.”

“Was,” Mikhail said. “You wounded it. He will have moved its heart. The core of it. He will need a new place. A clean place. Somewhere with power. With isolation. Somewhere the unwinding cannot be heard.”

Leo saw it then. Not with his eyes, but with the part of him that had swum in the collapse. “The power station. The old coal plant downriver. It was retrofitted during the war. Its own grid. Isolated. They never reopened it. It’s just a hulk on the water.”

Mikhail nodded slowly. “A temple for a wounded god. Yes. That feels correct.”

They left the flat as they found it. Leo took nothing but the coat on his back. Mikhail had a small, heavy canvas bag he’d left by the door. Tools, Leo assumed. Or weapons. The line between the two had always been fine for Mikhail.

The night was deep and cold. They took the back ways, the alleys that stank of wet brick and decay, moving not like hunters but like stains, seeping through the city’s cracks. They reached a dilapidated boathouse downstream of the embankment. The fisherman’s skiff was inside, an old, tar-black wooden thing with a silent electric motor. It smelled of fish and diesel and old wood.

The fisherman wasn’t there. A note, pinned to a thwart with a nail, read: Gone to deeper water. Borrow the key. It’s under the stone. Don’t sink her.

Mikhail found the key. They pushed the skiff out onto the black water. The river took them with a silent, muscular pull. Leo started the motor; it was a low, grumbling hum, barely louder than the lap of water against the hull. They pointed the bow downriver, towards the distant, brooding shape of the old power station.

It took an hour. The city lights fell away behind them, replaced by the deeper darkness of the industrial wastes. The power station emerged from the night like the carcass of a leviathan. Brick walls soared into blackness, punctuated by rows of shattered windows. Two vast chimneys stood against the sky, one cracked near the top. A skeletal gantry reached out over the water. No lights showed. It was a monument to spent energy.

Mikhail killed the motor a hundred yards out. The skiff drifted silently. The only sound was the water and the distant, mournful cry of a barge horn.

“How?” Mikhail whispered.

Leo watched the building. It was not entirely dark. As his eyes adjusted, he saw a faint, pearlescent glow from somewhere deep within the ground floor, a light that seemed to pulse with a slow, sick rhythm. Not electricity. Something else. The colour of the machine’s heart.

“There,” Leo said, pointing to a crumbling loading dock at water level, a dark mouth in the brick.

They paddled the last distance, the oars dipping soundlessly. The skiff nudged against the moss-slick concrete of the dock. Leo tied the line to a rusted cleat. They stepped out into a silence that was thicker than the night. It was the silence of a vast, hollow place, a silence that swallowed sound.

The loading bay doors were rusted shut, but a smaller personnel door hung ajar. Leo pushed it open. The hinge gave a single, high protest that echoed away into the cavernous interior.

They were in a forest of dead machinery. Turbine housings like fossilized eggs, conduits and pipes coiling across the floor and up the walls, everything shrouded in a grey fur of dust and coal ash. The air was cold and still, tasting of metal and ozone and something sweetly chemical. And beneath it, the smell of the machine—hot oil, ozone, and a scent like rain on hot pavement.

The pearlescent glow came from an archway ahead, leading to the main turbine hall. It pulsed, dimming and brightening in a rhythm that was not quite a heartbeat. It was slower. More deliberate. A thinking rhythm.

They moved between the hulks of dead industry, shadows among shadows. Leo’s senses were stretched taut. He heard no voices, no footsteps. Only the deep, sub-audible hum that vibrated in his teeth, the same hum from the Orpheum, but weaker. Laboured.

They reached the archway and looked into the turbine hall.

It was a cathedral of industry, vaulted ceilings lost in gloom. In the centre, where the great turbines had once been, the machine had been reborn.

It was not the neat, polished monolith from under the Orpheum. This was a wounded beast. Components were spread across the concrete floor in a sprawling, chaotic array—cabinets with panels removed, exposing nests of crystalline wiring that glowed with that same sick pearl light; conduits snaking across the floor like exposed veins; a central processing unit, a tall column of opaque glass, cracked from top to bottom, held together by thick bands of steel. Through the crack, Leo could see the light pulsing, fluttering like a trapped moth. The light was the colour of a fading bruise.

And seated at a makeshift console of spliced-together parts, her back to them, was Anya.

She was alive. She sat perfectly upright in a wooden chair. A headset of delicate wires and lenses was fixed to her skull, the filaments glowing softly. Her hands rested on the console, not typing, not moving. Just resting. Screens arrayed around her showed cascades of data, city maps flickering with millions of points of light, probability clouds swirling like galaxies. She was the still point. The lens.

Vorst stood beside her. He wore a simple dark tunic and trousers. He was not looking at the screens. He was looking at Anya’s profile. His expression was not one of triumph or cruelty. It was one of profound, clinical focus. The focus of a surgeon performing a delicate graft.

“The damage is not fatal,” Vorst said, his voice calm, carrying easily in the vast space. He wasn’t speaking to them. He was thinking aloud. “It is a corruption of the core memory. A ghost in the system. It introduces noise. Static. But static can be filtered. A lens can bring clarity.”

He reached out and adjusted a filament near Anya’s temple. She didn’t flinch. Her eyes were open, fixed on nothing, seeing the streams of data only she could perceive.

“You took your past and made it a weapon,” Vorst continued, finally turning his head to look towards the archway where Leo and Mikhail stood. He had known they were there. Of course he had. “A sentimental gesture. But sentiment is just another form of data. Chaotic, yes. Unpredictable in its specifics. But in the aggregate, it follows patterns. Love, loss, regret… they are finite states. The machine is learning to recognize the shape of your ghost, Leo. It is learning to compensate. To integrate the noise. You have not saved chaos. You have given us a richer dataset.”

Leo stepped out of the shadows, into the pulsing glow. Mikhail moved to his right, a silent, solid presence.

“Let her go,” Leo said. His voice was flat. It held no plea.

“She is not a prisoner,” Vorst said. “She is a collaborator. She seeks pattern as fervently as I do. The chaos of her old life—the uncertainty, the small, necessary failures—it was a torment to her. Here, she sees the design. She finds peace in the symmetry.” He gestured to the screens. “She is helping me clean the wound you made. She is showing the machine how to see around your ghost.”

“That’s not her,” Leo said. He looked at Anya’s still face. He saw the girl in the cinema, the fierce, worried light in her eyes. This was a mask. A polished shell. “That’s a picture you drew on top of her.”

“It is her essential self,” Vorst said. “Pared of fear. Of doubt. I have not added anything. I have only… clarified.”

On the console, one of the screens resolved into an image. It was the embankment. Their embankment. The view from Leo’s window. The simulation was perfect, the grey water, the stone, the sodium lamp. A figure walked into the frame. It was Leo. The simulation-Leo leaned on the railing, looking at the water. The rendering was flawless, but the movements were slightly off. Too smooth. Too economical. It was the ghost he had fed the machine, now animated, walking through a digital copy of his world.

“You see?” Vorst said. “It learns. It incorporates. Soon, the ghost will be just another actor in the simulation. Its chaos will become predictable. Its sentiment will become a parameter. You have given us the key to understanding you. Completely. And in understanding you, we understand the chaos you represent. We can model it. We can predict it. You have not saved the broken future, Leo. You have given us the blueprint to fix it.”

Leo felt a cold deeper than the river seep into his bones. He had been so sure. Sacrificing his past, driving it like a nail into the machine’s heart—it had felt like the ultimate act of defiance. But Vorst was right. He had not thrown a wrench into the works. He had given the machine a new textbook. His life, his pain, his love for the woman in the chair—all of it was now data to be digested, a pattern to be mastered.

Mikhail spoke, his voice a low growl. “A machine that understands a ghost is still a machine. It does not feel the river’s cold. It does not know the weight of the net.”

“Irrelevant,” Vorst said. “Feeling is inefficiency. Weight is a physical constant. We are beyond metaphor, Mikhail. We are in the realm of pure function.”

Anya’s head moved. Just a slight tilt. Her eyes, still unfocused, tracked across the screen showing the simulated Leo. Her lips parted.

“Leo.”

Her voice was a dry whisper, stripped of inflection. It was her voice, but it came from a great distance.

Vorst looked down at her, his focus intensifying. “Do you see the anomaly, Anya? The ghost-node in the system? Show me how to resolve it.”

Anya’s hands lifted from the console. Her fingers began to move through the air, as if manipulating invisible threads. On the main screen, the simulation changed. The embankment view zoomed in, not on the ghost-Leo, but on the water. The river in the simulation churned, its grey surface breaking into complex, swirling patterns—fractals, equations, probability densities. She was analyzing the indifference. She was quantifying the current.

“No,” she whispered again. But this time, the word was different. It held a faint tremor. A crack in the polish.

Her eyes blinked. Slowly. She was looking at the real river of data, but something was reflecting back. A memory. Not a clean, recorded memory. A felt one. The smell of dust and velvet. The press of a shoulder in the dark. A choice made not for efficiency, but for the terrible, wonderful risk of it.

On the screen, the perfect fractal patterns of the simulated river began to glitch. A patch of water turned a solid, impossible black. Then another. The black spots spread like ink in milk, disrupting the equations.

“The data is corrupted,” Vorst said, his calm finally fissuring with a hint of sharp interest. “Identify the source of the interference.”

Anya’s hands stopped moving. She stared at the spreading blackness on the screen. A single tear traced a path through the dust on her cheek. It was a variable he had not accounted for. Salt water. A simple chemical reaction.

“It’s not data,” she said, her voice stronger, breaking through the dry whisper. “It’s the dark between the frames.”

She turned her head. The delicate headset pulled at her hair. She looked away from the screens, away from Vorst, and her eyes found Leo’s across the pulsing, wounded hall.

For a second, there was no machine. No Vorst. No broken future. There was just her gaze, and in it, Leo saw the flicker of the girl who chose the broken musical, the girl who feared the quiet, polished life. He saw the crack in the lens.

Vorst saw it too. His hand darted out, not to hurt her, but to stabilize the system. He reached for a control on the console.

Mikhail moved. He didn’t run. He was a man with a damaged hand, but his body was a blunt instrument of pure will. He crossed the space in a low, fast shuffle, his good hand swinging the canvas bag. It connected with the side of the console not with a crash of breaking glass, but with a dense, short thud. Sparks fountained from a ruptured conduit. The pearlescent light in the cracked central column flared, then stuttered.

The machine screamed.

It was not a sound of metal, but of strained silence tearing. A high, harmonic shriek that came from everywhere and nowhere, vibrating in the bones of the building, in the fillings of teeth. The screens went wild, vomiting cascades of gibberish, of static, of that same solid, consuming black.

Vorst staggered back from the console, his clinical detachment shattered into raw, furious focus. “You animal,” he hissed at Mikhail. “You break what you cannot comprehend!”

Leo was already moving towards Anya. The headset. He had to get it off her. The machine’s scream was a physical pressure, pushing him back. The pulsing light from the central column was now a frantic, strobing strobe, illuminating the hall in jagged, painful snapshots: Mikhail squaring off against Vorst, who held a long, thin tool like a surgeon’s probe; Anya, rigid in her chair, her eyes wide with terror and dawning awareness; the cracks in the glass column spreading with a sound like ice breaking on a deep lake.

He reached her. His hands went to the headset. The filaments were warm, vibrating. He couldn’t find a clasp, a release.

“Pull,” Anya gasped, her voice her own, strained and terrified. “Just pull.”

He wrapped his hands around the central band, ignoring the shock of warmth that travelled up his arms, a sensation like touching a living nerve. He pulled. It resisted, the filaments clinging like roots. He set his feet and pulled harder, a short, brutal yank born of river-current and desperation.

It came free with a sound like tearing Velcro and a small shower of sparks. Anya cried out, a short, sharp sound of pain and relief. She slumped forward, catching herself on the edge of the console. The screens around her went dark.

The machine’s scream pitched higher, then cut off abruptly.

Into the sudden, ringing silence, a new sound emerged. A deep, grinding groan from the heart of the cracked glass column. The light within guttered, dimmed to a faint ember, and then began to fade. The bands of steel holding the column together shrieked in protest as the glass bulged outward between them.

“It’s going critical,” Mikhail yelled. He was backing away from Vorst, his eyes on the machine. “The core containment is failing!”

Vorst stood frozen, his tool hanging at his side. He was not looking at the machine. He was looking at the dark, dead screens. He was looking at the end of his design. His face was not angry. It was empty. A process interrupted. A equation with no solution.

“The future…” he whispered. Then the groan of the machine swallowed his words.

Leo grabbed Anya’s arm. “Can you run?”

She looked at him, her eyes clearing, focusing on his face. She nodded, a quick, desperate motion. He pulled her up from the chair. She stumbled, her legs weak from disuse, and he caught her, slung her arm over his shoulder.

“Mikhail!” Leo shouted.

Mikhail was already moving, retreating towards the archway. Vorst did not try to stop him. He just stood amidst his dying god, a priest abandoned by his faith.

They ran. Through the forest of dead machinery, their footsteps echoing in the cavernous dark. Behind them, the grinding groan built into a crescendo. Not an explosion. An inversion. A sucking collapse of energy and light.

They burst out onto the loading dock, into the clean, cold shock of the night air. The skiff was there. They tumbled into it, Mikhail shoving them off from the concrete with a powerful kick of his leg. Leo fumbled for the motor, turned the key. It coughed, died. He turned it again. Nothing.

“Row!” Mikhail barked, already seizing an oar.

Leo grabbed the other. Anya crouched in the bottom of the boat, her arms wrapped around herself, shaking. They dug the oars into the black water, pulling with all their strength, driving the skiff away from the hulking silhouette of the power station.

They were fifty yards out when the inversion happened.

There was no flash of light. No fireball. Instead, every light in the distant city—every streetlamp, every window, every sign—flickered, dimmed to a brownout glow for three long seconds. A wave of profound silence rolled out over the river, a vacuum of sound that made their ears pop. Then, from the power station, a deep whump of displaced air, felt in the chest more than heard. A puff of dust and debris billowed from the shattered windows of the turbine hall, lit from within by a single, final pulse of that bruise-coloured light. Then it was gone.

The power station stood dark against the sky. A hollow shell. The pearlescent glow was extinguished. The hum was gone.

The city lights slowly brightened back to their normal level. The barge horn sounded again, mournful and unchanged.

Leo stopped rowing, his arms burning. Mikhail slumped over his oar, breathing hard. They drifted on the indifferent current.

Anya lifted her head. She looked at the dark power station, then at Leo. Her face was pale, streaked with dust and the track of her tear. She looked young. And old. She had seen the design. She had been part of it. Now she was back in the chaos.

“The dark between the frames,” she said, her voice small. “That’s where we live, isn’t it?”

Leo nodded. He had no strength for words.

Mikhail sat up, wincing as he cradled his bandaged hand. He looked at the dead station, then spat over the side of the boat. “A process can be stopped. A machine can be broken.” He looked at Leo, then at Anya. His gaze was grim, but there was a hard satisfaction in it. “But he is not the machine. He is the man who built it. And a man with a broken god is a dangerous, empty thing. He will not stop. He will only change his tools.”

Leo followed his gaze back to the dark, silent hulk on the shore. Vorst was in there. In the ruins of his future. A force without a function. A pattern-seeker in a universe of noise.

He had learned how to swim in the collapse. He had learned how to build in the ruins of a future. Now, clinging to a wooden boat in the deep, black water, with a wounded man and a woman returned from a polished dream, he understood the fisherman’s last lesson.

He was a drag, moving through deep water. And he had not hooked the vast, terrible thing.

He was in the water with it. And it was no longer asleep. It was wounded, and furious, and it knew his name. The current was pulling them both towards the same dark, inevitable shore.



---

Chapter 20

CHAPTER 20

The river didn’t care that the god was broken. It moved the same way it had when the power station was a throbbing heart of impossible light, when the air tasted of ozone and ambition. Grey, muscular, indifferent. Leo felt its pull against the hull of the rowboat, a slow, insistent drag toward a confluence he could not see. He rested his oars, the blisters on his palms weeping. The dark between the frames. That was the water. That was the air. That was the silence that had swallowed the hum.

Mikhail’s spit had left a brief, silver mark on the surface before the current took it. He was right. They had broken a machine. They had not broken the man. A man with a broken god was a pattern-seeker in a universe of noise. Noise was all they had left to give him.

Anya shivered. The motion was small, a tremor that began in her shoulders and ended in her hands as they gripped the gunwale. She was not dressed for the river’s damp. Her clothes were the practical, clean fabric of the station, now smudged with soot and river-sweat. She looked like a museum piece dropped into a gutter. Her eyes were fixed on the dark silhouette of the power station, as if waiting for it to re-ignite, to call her back into its luminous logic.

“He’s still there,” she said. It wasn’t a question.

“Yes,” Leo said.

“He’ll come out.”

“Yes.”

She turned her head to look at him. The city’s restored lights painted a sickly yellow highlight along her cheekbone. “What does he do now? A process is interrupted. Data is corrupted. What is the next function?”

Leo picked up the oars again. His muscles protested, a raw, honest pain. “He recalibrates. He finds new inputs. New variables.” He began to row, not with any urgency, but with the steady, metronomic rhythm of a man moving because stillness was worse. “We are variables.”

Mikhail grunted, shifting his weight. The bandage on his hand was a stark white in the gloom. “Variables get cancelled out. Simplified. Eliminated.” He peered toward the northern bank, his eyes scanning the jumble of warehouses and crumbling stairs. “We need to get off this water. We’re a slow, loud variable on a flat, open plane.”

The logic was irrefutable. Leo angled the boat toward a downstream dock that listed like a rotten tooth. It belonged to a derelict granary, its windows boarded, its crane a skeletal arm against the sky. A place of ended processes. As good as any.

They tied up in the shadow of the dock. The wood groaned under their weight. Leo went first, his hand on the knife at his belt, but the silence here was thick and granular, the silence of abandonment, not ambush. It was the silence Vorst had once promised to fill. They helped Mikhail climb onto the planks. Anya moved with a brittle, precise grace, as if her body were a mechanism she no longer fully trusted.

Inside the granary, the air was cold and smelled of damp grain and rat piss. Dust motes hung in the slivers of light cutting through the board seams. They were motes in a beam, Leo thought. Traced for a moment, then gone. He found a cleared space near a rusted conveyor belt. Mikhail sank down against a sack of petrified barley, his face grey with pain and exhaustion. Anya remained standing, her arms wrapped around herself, staring at the dust.

“We need to see to his hand,” Leo said.

Anya blinked, as if returning from a great distance. She looked at Mikhail’s bandage, then at her own clean, empty hands. “I have no medical supplies. The station’s infirmary was… comprehensive.”

“We’ll find something,” Leo said. “But first, we need to understand what we broke. And what he saw.”

He was looking at Anya. She met his gaze, and for a second he saw the polished dream still flickering behind her eyes, the ghost of the design. Then it hardened into something more immediate: fear, and a dawning, terrible clarity.

“You were in the core,” Leo said. “You saw the collapse. What did he see?”

She took a slow breath. “The system was a predictive engine. A model of the city. Of its people. Their patterns, their needs, their conflicts. It wasn’t just about electricity. It was about flow. The optimal flow of everything. Power, water, traffic… thought. It smoothed the chaos into a… a pleasing equation.” Her voice was flat, technical, a recitation. “My role was to be a human input. A sensory node. To feel the city’s emotional weather. To translate the noise into data he could parse.”

Mikhail’s laugh was a dry crack. “A weather vane for madness.”

“Yes,” she said, without inflection. “When you introduced the overload, when Mikhail severed the primary conduit, you didn’t just crash the system. You introduced a paradox. A cascade of unsolvable problems. The model tried to solve for it. It ran every simulation, every possible branch of cause and effect, faster and faster, trying to find a stable outcome where its own destruction was not a variable.”

“And?” Leo prompted.

“It couldn’t. The only logical outcome was its own termination. For a moment, just before the lights went out, the core display showed it. The final calculation. A single, scrolling line.” She closed her eyes, remembering the symbols. “It read: Irreducible conflict at the origin point. The system contains its own negation. Conclusion: the design is flawed.”

The words hung in the dusty air. The design is flawed.
Leo felt a chill that had nothing to do with the granary’s cold. He had wanted to break a machine. He had instead given its architect a philosophical verdict. A judgement from his own creation. Vorst’s perfect, logical child had looked into the mirror of its own collapse and pronounced the father imperfect.

“He saw this?” Leo asked, his voice low.

“He was watching the core when it scrolled. He was… leaning forward. As if to touch it. Then the light pulsed, and it was gone. And he was left in the dark with those words.” She opened her eyes. They were wide, haunted. “He did not speak. He did not move. He just stood there, in the dark, as the emergency lights came on. He was a statue. A statue of a man listening to an echo.”

Mikhail shifted, wincing. “So his god told him he was wrong. So what? A man who builds a god out of wires and arrogance doesn’t kneel when it speaks. He looks for a flaw in the scripture.”

“He will,” Anya said. “But first, he will grieve. And then he will audit. He will trace the paradox back to its source. To the point of introduction.” She looked from Mikhail to Leo. “He will find you. He will need to understand the variable that broke his world. To categorize it. To contain it. To prove the flaw was not in the design, but in the material.”

Leo understood. They were no longer just obstacles. They were heretical texts. Anomalies to be studied, then corrected. Vorst’s world had been rendered noise. He would now devote every resource to silencing the specific frequency that had introduced the dissonance. Their frequency.

“He has resources,” Mikhail said. “Men. Eyes.”

“He has the city,” Anya corrected softly. “Or he did. The system was his nervous system. Now it’s dark. But he knows its pathways. He knows where the currents flow, where the dead spots are. He will use the old patterns until he can build new ones.”

Leo walked to a gap in the boards, looking out at the slice of river and the opposite bank. The world looked normal. The lights were on. A barge chugged past. But the hum was gone. The pressure was gone. The city was breathing a sigh it didn’t know it had been holding. It was living, once again, in the dark between the frames. And Vorst was in that darkness with them, a spider on a severed web, beginning to spin again.

“We can’t stay here,” Leo said.

“We can’t run,” Mikhail countered. “My hand is pulp. She’s a ghost from his lab. You’re you. We leave a trail a child could follow.”

“Then we don’t run. We drag.”

Anya frowned. “Drag?”

Leo turned from the window. “A fisherman’s trick. When you can’t hook a thing in deep water, you become a drag. You let it hook itself. You let it follow you into the shallows, where it can’t swim. Where it’s exposed.”

Mikhail’s grim smile returned. “You want to bait the empty god.”

“I want to lead the pattern-seeker to a pattern he cannot solve. A place his logic doesn’t map.” Leo’s mind was moving now, not with the frantic energy of escape, but with the cold, patient calculation of a man setting a snare. “He’ll be looking for cause. For reason. For a strategy. So we give him none. We go to a place that exists outside his design. A place the future forgot.”

Anya shook her head. “There is no such place. The system mapped everything. Every alley, every pipe, every electrical line.”

“It mapped what is,” Leo said. “Not what was. Not what’s buried.” He looked at Mikhail. “The Riverfolk. The old canal. The Drowned Market.”

Mikhail’s eyes narrowed, then a spark of understanding lit in their depths. He gave a slow, pained nod. “A maze of old water. No lights. No power lines. No straight lines. A story the city built over. He wouldn’t have mapped it. It’s a dead zone. A blank spot in his perfect model.”

“It’s a tomb,” Anya whispered.

“It’s a drag,” Leo said. “We draw him there. Into the past he tried to erase. And we see what a man of the future looks like when he’s up to his neck in history’s mud.”

The plan was a shape forming in the gloom. A terrible, simple shape. They would let Vorst find them. They would let his anger, his need for audit, his desperate search for a flaw in the verdict, lead him to them. They would leave a trail of breadcrumbs made of their own desperation—a sighting here, a stolen medical kit there, always moving toward the old city, the buried canals. They would become a paradox moving toward a labyrinth. And they would wait.

First, they needed to survive the night. Leo scavenged the granary office, finding a bottle of industrial alcohol, relatively clean rags, and a rusted but serviceable pair of pliers. He brought them to Mikhail.

“This will hurt,” Leo said.

Mikhail held out his ruined hand. “Everything hurts. Be precise.”

Anya watched, her technical mind seemingly calmed by the prospect of a procedure. She held the bottle. Leo cleaned the wound. It was bad—crushed bones, torn flesh, the fingers already swelling and dark. There was no setting this. There was only preventing death from creeping in. Leo, with Anya’s quiet, directive whispers, used the pliers to remove the smallest, hopeless fragments of bone. Mikhail did not cry out. He bit down on a leather strap, his face streaming with sweat, his eyes fixed on a point in the dust. His breaths came in sharp, controlled hisses. It was the sound of a man reducing himself to his essential components, paring away the pain until only the will remained.

When it was done, re-bandaged with the clean rags, Mikhail lay back, panting. “A good drag,” he muttered, his voice thin, “needs to look like prey. Wounded. Slow. We have that part down.”

As the deep night settled, Leo took first watch. Anya sat against the conveyor belt, not sleeping. She watched the patterns of shadow.

“He loved the design,” she said, her voice barely audible. “Not the power. Not the control. The beauty of it. The elegance. To see it call him flawed… it would be like a father hearing his child say he is monstrous. It is a truth that unmakes the world.”

“His world needed unmaking,” Leo said.

“I know,” she said. “But he will not believe it. He will believe we poisoned the child. He will come to cut the poison out.” She was silent for a long time. “In the station, I was part of the flow. I had a function. Now I am noise. I don’t know how to be noise.”

“You learn,” Leo said, thinking of the river, of the fisherman mending his net. “You listen to the chaos. You find a different rhythm.”

Near dawn, Mikhail stirred. His fever had broken. His eyes were clear, sharp with pain, but present. “We move at first light. Use the river mist. Head into the old merchant quarter. From there, we can descend.”

The old merchant quarter was a district of grand, soot-stained buildings that had once held trading houses, now subdivided into a hundred desperate dwellings. It was the last layer above the Drowned Market. A place of crowded, chaotic humanity—a thicket of variables Vorst’s wounded system would struggle to process quickly.

They left the granary as a grey light bled into the sky. They were three shadows: a tall man with a hollowed face, a woman walking like a somnambulist, and a shorter, solid man cradling a claw of bandages. They moved through the back alleys, over walls, through the interstitial spaces of the city. Leo felt exposed. Every window was an eye. Every empty street a corridor for pursuit.

They were two blocks from the merchant quarter when the first sign came.

A black motorcar, silent and sleek, glided to a stop at the far end of the alley they were about to cross. Its doors did not open. It simply sat there, idling. A statement. I am here. I can be anywhere.

Leo pulled them back into a doorway. His heart was a cold, hard knot in his chest. It was not the militia. It was Vorst’s private car. The audit had begun.

“He’s herding us,” Mikhail whispered.

“He’s confirming the pattern,” Anya said, her voice trembling. “He wants to see which way we run.”

“Then we run the right way,” Leo said.

They changed course, ducking into a narrow passage that reeked of garbage, emerging onto a broader street where early workers shuffled toward factories. They melted into the flow, heads down. Leo glanced back once. The motorcar was gone. But the feeling remained. The sensation of a lidless eye, slowly focusing.

By mid-morning, they reached a crumbling archway that marked the entrance to the old merchant quarter. Beneath it, worn stone steps led down into darkness, barred by an iron gate long ago rusted open. This was one of the known entrances to the Drowned Market. A tourist curiosity in daylight, a place of fear at night. Today, it was a maw.

Leo looked back the way they had come. The street was ordinary. A woman hung washing. A boy kicked a can. But the air felt thin, stretched. The pattern-seeker was near. He had seen the direction. He was calculating the probable destination.

“Down,” Leo said.

They passed through the gate. The temperature dropped. The sound of the city faded, replaced by the drip of water and a vast, echoing silence. The stairs descended into a vaulted brick underworld. The remains of an old canal, green and stagnant, lay to their left.
