To their right, raised stone walkways stretched into gloom, fronted by the fossilized facades of shops and taverns from a century past. Windows were boarded with rotten wood. Signs were faded ghosts. It was a city in negative, a photographic plate of a forgotten time. The air was thick with the smell of stagnant water, mildew, and time.

The Drowned Market.
Vorst’s future had no map for this.

They moved in, the weak light from the entrance fading behind them. Leo lit a small electric torch he’d taken from the granary. Its beam was a frail blade, cutting through the darkness, revealing glimpses of a lost world: a porcelain doll’s face grinning from a silt-filled booth, a rusted cash register, the ribs of a rotting boat.

They found a relatively dry alcove, a former café, its tables and chairs now just skeletal outlines. They could rest here. They could wait.

Hours passed. The silence was absolute. It was not a peaceful silence. It was the silence of a sunken ship. Anya sat on the stone counter, her knees drawn up. Mikhail slept fitfully, his breath rasping. Leo watched the darkness where the entrance had been.

He thought of Vorst, up in the light, marshaling his resources. He would not come himself. Not at first. He would send probes. Men with lights and guns. He would try to map the unmappable. He would fail. And then his anger, his need to confront the anomaly, to look it in the eye and prove it was the flaw, not him… that would draw him down.

The drag was set.

It was Mikhail who heard it first. His eyes snapped open. He held up his good hand for silence.

A sound. From deep within the maze of canals. Not from the entrance.

A soft, mechanical whir. Then a click.

It was a sound that did not belong. A sound of precision, of maintained parts, of the present.

Leo extinguished his torch. The darkness became total, a physical substance. He heard Anya’s sharp intake of breath.

Another whir. Closer. To their left.

It was not men. Men would splash, would whisper, would curse the dark.

This was something else. Something Vorst had built for the dark between the frames.

Leo’s hand found his knife. He strained his eyes. He saw nothing.

Then, a hundred feet down the canal, a single, cold blue eye winked on. It was a small light, mounted on a smooth, dark housing. It swept across the water, then up, scanning the facades. It moved with a smooth, inhuman patience.

It was an auditor. A mechanical eye sent to find the flaw in the silence.

The blue eye passed over their alcove, hesitated, and moved on. The whirring faded.

They had been scanned. They were now a data point.

Vorst was not just following them into the past.

He was bringing the future with him.

The dark around Leo felt different now. It was no longer just empty. It was instrumented. It was a laboratory. And they were the specimen under the lens.

The drag was in the water. And the vast, terrible thing was not just following.

It was sending its senses ahead, learning the shape of the deep, dark water for the first time.



---

Chapter 21

CHAPTER 21

The blue eye did not return. The whirring did not come back. The silence that followed was not the same silence. It was a silence that had been measured, quantified, and filed away. It was the silence of a room after a stranger has looked in, noted the contents, and closed the door without a word. It held the memory of the intrusion.

Leo kept his torch dark. The darkness was no longer a refuge. It was a medium. A conductive gel for signals they could not hear. He stared into the black where the light had been, his eyes making nothing, his mind making everything. A smooth housing. A precise sweep. No heat signature, no breath, no fear. A thing built for this. Vorst had not waited for the drag to pull him. He had sent a probe on a taut, invisible line ahead of him. He was mapping the darkness. He was turning the labyrinth into a known quantity. The thought was colder than the water.

Mikhail’s breathing was a shallow rasp. “An auditor,” he whispered. The word was a curse. “He’s auditing the anomaly.”

“He saw us,” Anya said. Her voice was flat. It was not a question.

“It registered us,” Leo corrected. He did not look at her. He kept his eyes on the dark. “We are a deviation in the expected data set. A fluctuation in the ambient silence. Now he knows the fluctuation exists. He will want to understand its source.”

He thought of the man mending the net on the embankment. The small failures, the patient corrections. Vorst was the same. The probe was his first cast. It had found a snag. The next cast would be different. The correction would be applied.

“We move,” Leo said.

“Where?” Mikhail’s voice was tight with pain. “It came from the deep. It’s ahead of us.”

“It came from a deep,” Leo said. He stood, his joints protesting. The damp had settled into them, a slow, claiming chill. “This place is a knot. Threads cross. We cut sideways.”

He helped Mikhail up. The old man’s weight was a troubling, boneless thing. Anya gathered their meager pack, her movements silent. They waded into the canal, the water clutching at their thighs. Leo led them away from the direction of the blue eye, choosing a narrow offshoot to the right. The brickwork here was older, the mortar a crumbling paste. The ceiling dipped so low they had to crouch. They moved by touch and by the faint, sourceless phosphorescence that wept from patches of wet stone. It was not light to see by. It was light to remind you that you were blind.

They walked for an hour, or what felt like an hour. Time in the dark was a liquid, pooling in some stretches, rushing in others. The canals branched and intersected. Leo chose turns at random, but his randomness had a pattern: away from the wider channels, toward the constricted, the forgotten. He was thinking like a flaw in the data. A flaw did not take the logical path. It spiked. It ghosted. It existed where it should not.

They found a chamber where the water spread into a shallow, stagnant pool. The remains of a brick arch lay collapsed in the water, half-submerged like the spine of a dead beast. They climbed onto a dry ledge at the far side. Leo risked the torch. The beam was a violation. It showed them a space no larger than a prison cell, walls slick with mineral deposits that formed jagged, tooth-like structures. It was a cavity in the city’s jaw. A place to trap food.

Mikhail sank down, his back against the wall. His face was the colour of the mortar. Anya checked his bandages. The fabric was soaked through with something that was not just water.

“He needs light,” she said. “He needs dry.”

“He needs not to be found,” Leo said. But he held the torch steady, its light a small, guilty sun in the dripping dark.

Anya worked. She had a thief’s hands, quick and sure. When she peeled the bandage back, Leo saw the wound. It was not clean. The flesh around the puncture was swollen, a livid purple. It smelled of spoilage and the deep, organic decay of the canals. It was the smell of the past, and it was eating Mikhail from the inside.

“It’s fouled,” Anya said. Her clinical tone was a thin veneer over a deep well of fear. “The water here… it’s poison.”

Mikhail chuckled, a dry, papery sound. “The past is septic. I told you.”

“We need antibiotics,” she said, looking at Leo as if he could conjure them from the stone.

Leo said nothing. He had a knife. He had a torch. He had the clothes on his back and the relentless, grey weight of the river in his mind. He had no medicine. Medicine belonged to the world of maintained parts, of precision. It belonged to Vorst.

“Clean it. Bind it tight,” he said. “It’s all we have.”

Anya’s jaw tightened, but she nodded. She rinsed the wound with a scant few drops from their canteen, then bound it with the last of the clean cloth. It was a gesture. A ritual against the inevitable. When she was done, she sat back, hugging her knees. The torchlight carved hollows under her eyes.

“What was that thing?” she asked. She was not looking at Leo. She was looking at the black water of the pool.

“A sensor,” Leo said. “Eyes for a man who won’t come down to see for himself.”

“It’s more than that,” Mikhail murmured. His eyes were closed. “An auditor doesn’t just look. It listens. For echoes that shouldn’t be there. For stories the walls aren’t telling. It’s building a model. A perfect, hollow model of this place. So he can find the imperfection. So he can cut it out.”

The whirring. The click. The scanning eye. It was listening for the silence they made. Leo understood. They were not just hiding. They were a dissonant chord in a song of perfect silence. Vorst’s machine was tuning the instrument.

“Then we make more noise,” Anya said.

Leo looked at her.

“We’re a flaw,” she said, a hard edge in her voice. “So we act like one. We don’t just hide in the wrong place. We do the wrong thing. We break his model.”

A faint, approving sound came from Mikhail. It might have been a laugh, or a gasp of pain.

Leo considered the dark. He considered the patient, mechanical mind that was even now processing the data of their presence. A model was a prediction. It expected continuity. It expected a hiding thing to continue hiding. A fleeing thing to continue fleeing.

“What is the wrong thing?” he asked.

Anya met his gaze. “We stop running. We go toward the eye.”

It was madness. It was the kind of move that only made sense on the edge of a knife. It was spiking the data so hard it broke the sensor. Leo felt the old, cold calculus click into place. It was not a plan for survival. It was a plan for confrontation. A plan to turn the specimen into a pathogen.

“He’ll send more than an eye next time,” Leo said.

“I know,” said Anya.

Mikhail opened his eyes. They were fever-bright. “The drag is set. The line is tight. He will come. But he will come where his model tells him to. Where the flaw should be. If we are not there… we are behind him. We are in his blind spot. The place his perfect model says is empty.”

It was a soldier’s thinking. A partisan’s thinking. You do not fight the patrol. You let it pass, and you shoot the officer in the back.

Leo extinguished the torch. The darkness rushed back in, complete, consummate. “We rest. A few hours. Then we backtrack. We find the path of the eye.”

They did not sleep. They sat in the dark, listening to the water drip and Mikhail’s ragged breath. Leo thought of the blue eye. Its cold, impartial light. It did not hate them. It did not fear them. It simply registered their existence as an error. There was a purity to that. A terrible, clean logic. In a way, it was the final expression of the world above: a world where every mystery was a problem to be solved, every shadow a space to be illuminated, every man a set of data points to be corrected. Vorst was not a monster. He was a mechanic. And the universe was a machine that had developed a troubling noise.

He must have drifted into a shallow, waking doze, because the sound, when it came, was woven into a dream of whirring gears and silent rivers.

It was a splash.

Not the random plink of falling water. A deliberate, weighty displacement.

It came from the pool.

Leo was instantly awake, his hand on his knife. He heard Anya’s breath stop. Mikhail was still.

Another splash. Closer. Something was moving in the water of the chamber. Something large.

It was not the mechanical whir. This was organic. Slow. Purposeful.

Leo strained his ears. He heard a wet, dragging sound. Something grating on the brick of the ledge. A smell reached him, cutting through the general miasma of decay. It was a smell of deep mud, of cold blood, of a biology that had never seen the sun.

The thing was climbing out of the pool.

It breathed. A thick, guttural inhalation, like water being sucked down a drain.

Leo’s mind rejected it. The auditor was one thing—a thing of the future, a tool. This was something else. This was a thing of the deep past, the unrecorded past that lived in the sump of the city. It was the consequence of their flight into the unmappable. They had not just escaped Vorst. They had fallen off the edge of the known world, and the things that lived here were coming to see what had fallen in.

He could see nothing. The darkness was absolute. But he could feel its presence. A bulk, displacing the cold air. A heat that was not warmth, but the absence of the chill.

Anya’s hand found his arm. Her fingers were iron.

The thing let out a low, bubbling exhalation. It was a sound of curiosity. It took a step. The scrape of something hard on stone. A shell? A talon?

Leo did not move. He did not breathe. He became a part of the wall. He felt Anya doing the same beside him. Mikhail was a statue of pain.

The thing moved past them. It was close enough that Leo felt the air stir against his face. The smell was overwhelming—a tidal flat at the end of time. It paused. He heard the wet intake of air again. It was scenting them.

For an endless second, there was only the sound of that terrible breathing and the hammer of his own blood in his ears.

Then, with another wet, dragging step, the thing moved on. It crossed the ledge and slipped back into the water of a far canal with a soft, final gurgle.

The silence returned, heavier than before.

Minutes passed. Leo’s muscles screamed from the tension.

“What,” Anya whispered, the word barely a shape in the air, “was that?”

“The local fauna,” Mikhail breathed. His voice was a thread. “The things that live between the frames. The city forgets, but the stone remembers. And sometimes… it grows something.”

“It knew we were here,” Leo said.

“It didn’t care,” Mikhail replied. “Or we are not its food. We are something else. A curiosity. A bit of driftwood.”

They were in a food web they did not understand. They were being evaluated by minds they could not comprehend. First the machine. Now the native. They were caught between two alien intelligences.

The encounter decided it. The wrong thing. The flaw in the data.

“Now,” Leo said, standing. His body was stiff, protesting. “We move. While the trail is still cold.”

They waded back into the water, leaving the chamber of teeth. Leo led them back the way they had come, navigating by memory and touch. He was retracing their steps, a ghost walking backward through its own flight. The phosphorescence guided them, a faint, mocking glow.

After another age of darkness, they found the wider canal where they had first seen the blue eye. The water here felt different. It felt watched. Leo paused, listening. He heard only the eternal drip. But the silence had a quality of held breath.

They turned, not away from the deep, but into it. Toward the source of the whirring.

The architecture changed. The bricks gave way to older stone, roughly hewn. The canals were straighter, more deliberate. They were in a older layer, a foundational stratum. The air grew colder. The sound of their movement seemed louder, swallowed less by the soft walls.

They found the first sign an hour later.

A mark on the wall, at head height. It was a small, scorched circle, perfectly round. The stone was glazed, vitrified. In the centre of the circle was a tiny, drilled hole.

Leo ran his finger over it. The stone was still faintly warm.

“A survey marker,” Mikhail said, leaning close. “He’s laying a grid. Triangulating.”

They were inside the grid. They were points on a map being drawn in real time.

They pressed on, more cautiously now. The canals became a catacomb, a geometric honeycomb of identical channels. Left, right, straight. It was a test of memory. Leo felt the beginnings of vertigo. This was not natural. This was built, but not for transport. For something else. For storage. Or for confinement.

The second sign was a sound. Not a whir. A hum. A low, pervasive frequency that vibrated in the teeth and the bone behind the ears. It was coming from up ahead. It was the sound of power. Of maintained parts operating in a place where nothing should operate.

Leo killed the torch. They moved toward the hum.

The canal ended in a sheer wall. But the hum was strongest here. Leo felt along the stone. His fingers found a seam. Not a crack. A perfect, straight vertical line. He followed it. Another seam, horizontal. A door. Camouflaged to look like the wall.

He pushed. It did not budge. He felt along the edge, looking for a latch, a mechanism. His fingers brushed something cold and smooth. Metal. A plate. As he touched it, a faint, blue light emanated from its edges, outlining the door. It was a biometric scanner. A lock from the world above, planted here in the stone.

Vorst was not just mapping. He was claiming. He was installing his own architecture into the anomaly.

Anya pointed. To the left of the door, the canal continued as a narrow, flooded tunnel. The hum was slightly fainter that way, but it was still present.

They took the tunnel. It was a tight squeeze, the water up to their chests. The hum grew louder, resolving into a chorus of subtle vibrations: the whine of a gyroscope, the thrum of a generator, the whisper of circulating fluid.

The tunnel opened suddenly.

They were in a chamber the size of a small warehouse. It was not old. It was new.

The walls were smooth, prefabricated alloy panels, riveted into place against the ancient stone. The floor was a grated metal platform, suspended over a deep, black sump. The air was dry, scrubbed of the canal’s miasma, and tasted of ozone and filtered circulation. Light came from long, cold-white strips on the ceiling, illuminating the space with a surgical glare.

In the centre of the chamber stood the apparatus.

It was a complex of interconnected modules and consoles, all sleek, dark metal and glass. Conduits and cables snaked across the floor, connecting to banks of humming servers stacked against one wall. In the middle of it all was a cylindrical tank, filled with a clear, viscous fluid. Suspended in the fluid was a model. A perfect, glowing, three-dimensional holographic map of the canal system. It was stunning in its detail. Leo could see every channel, every chamber, every arch they had passed through. It was the labyrinth, rendered in light and data.

And in the map, three small, pulsing red dots glowed.

One was stationary, deep in the maze. Their chamber from hours before. The point where the auditor had registered them.

The other two were moving. Slowly, erratically, but moving. Toward this very chamber.

They were the dots.

Vorst had not just found them. He had visualized them. He had turned their fear, their flight, their pain, into a clean, red signal on a map.

A figure stood before the tank, his back to them. He was tall, dressed in a grey, utilitarian uniform. His hair was steel-grey, cut close. His hands were clasped behind his back as he studied the glowing map. He watched the two red dots as a biologist might watch specimens in a maze.

It was Vorst.

He had come down after all. He had built his laboratory in the belly of the anomaly. He was not waiting for the drag to pull him. He was conducting the experiment himself.

Leo’s knife felt absurd in his hand. A toothpick against a starship.

Vorst spoke. His voice was calm, resonant, filling the sterile chamber. He did not turn.

“The error is not in the system,” he said, as if lecturing to an empty room. “The system is perfect. It is a closed loop. The error is an intrusion. A piece of the outside, trapped inside. It creates friction. Noise. It must be isolated. Studied. And then removed.”

He turned then. His face was lean, intelligent, devoid of malice. He looked at Leo and Anya standing dripping on the metal grate, at Mikhail slumped between them. He looked at them with the same dispassionate interest with which he had regarded the red dots.

“You are the noise,” he said. “Thank you for coming. You have saved the auditor a further trip. Your cooperation in the final phase of the audit will be noted.”

From the shadows behind the consoles, two figures stepped forward. They were not men. They were humanoid, but their movements were too fluid, too perfectly balanced. Their skins were a matte grey composite. They had no faces, only smooth ovals where faces should be, each centred with a single, dark lens. They were the next iteration. The collectors.

The drag was not in the water.

It was here. In this bright, clean room at the heart of the dark.

They had not escaped the future.

They had walked directly into its processing centre.



---

Chapter 22

CHAPTER 22

The river didn’t care about laboratories. It moved the same way it had when the world was simple water and stone, before men learned to carve signals from silence. Leo felt its absence here, in this dry, humming belly. The only currents were of data, flowing through transparent conduits in the walls, and of intention, cold and direct, flowing from the man in grey.

Vorst watched them. He did not smile. He did not frown. He assessed. His gaze moved from Leo’s knife to Anya’s arm locked around Mikhail’s waist, to Mikhail’s head lolling against his chest. It was the gaze of a clockmaker looking at three misaligned gears that had, against all probability, found their way into his immaculate machine.

The two grey collectors stepped forward. Their footfalls made no sound on the metal grate. They were not hurrying. There was no need. The maze was gone. This was the sorting table.

“The anomaly is a perfect receiver,” Vorst said, turning back to the tank. The red dots pulsed, a slow, patient heartbeat. “It hears the shape of things to come. The drag is not a pull, but a pattern. The future has a gravity, gentlemen. And some people… some events… are denser than others. They fall toward it sooner. My system merely listens to that gravity. It maps the fall.”

Leo’s hand ached around the knife handle. The blade was a sliver of the old world, of things that cut flesh and drew blood that was red and wet. It belonged to the riverbank, to the mender of nets with his patient corrections. It did not belong here, where people were reduced to luminous points and problems were solved with the quiet efficiency of a filter removing silt.

“You’re harvesting us,” Anya said. Her voice was flat, stripped of inflection. It was the voice of someone stating the weather.

“Harvesting implies a crop,” Vorst replied, his eyes still on the tank. “This is auditing. The future is a complex equation. Sentient variables introduce chaos. You are chaotic elements. Your presence in the stream creates eddies, contradictions. My function is to smooth the stream. To resolve the equation before it resolves itself in… messy ways.”

One of the collectors stopped a pace from Mikhail. Its smooth, lensed face tilted down. A thin, articulated probe, like the needle of a seamstress, extended silently from its wrist. It moved toward Mikhail’s temple.

Leo moved.

It was not a decision. It was a reflex, the useless core twitching. He stepped forward, placing himself between the probe and Mikhail. He raised the knife. The gesture was prehistoric.

Vorst sighed. It was a small, technical sound, like steam escaping a valve.

The collector did not pause. Its other hand flicked out. It was not a strike; it was a dismissal. The back of its grey composite hand connected with Leo’s wrist. There was a pressure, precise and overwhelming, and the knife was gone, clattering across the grate and into the shadows beneath a console. Leo’s arm went numb to the elbow. He stumbled back, colliding with Anya.

The probe touched Mikhail’s skin.

Mikhail jerked. A full-body spasm, as if he’d been plunged into ice. His eyes flew open. They were not seeing the room. They were wide, fixed on some internal panorama of terror. A low, ragged sound tore from his throat, not a scream, but the scrape of a soul against a bare surface.

On the tank, the two red dots flared. Their light deepened, throbbing from a signal into a warning. A third point of light, fainter, a pale amber, flickered into existence near them, then vanished.

Vorst leaned closer to the glass. “Fascinating. The variable’s resistance amplifies the signal. The interference pattern becomes clearer.”

The probe retracted. Mikhail sagged, a marionette with cut strings. His breathing was a shallow, rapid flutter. The collector’s lens focused on Leo and Anya next.

“You will come,” Vorst said, not looking at them. “The holding cells are adjacent. You will be comfortable. The process is not painful. It is a… disentanglement.”

The second collector moved toward Anya. Its hands, with their subtle, tool-tipped fingers, opened.

Anya did not look at the thing approaching her. She looked past it, at the wall of consoles, at the streams of light in the conduits. Her face was pale, but her eyes were calculating. She was not accepting the weather. She was reading the horizon.

“Your system listens,” she said, her voice cutting the sterile air. “What does it hear when the things it’s listening to… listen back?”

Vorst went very still. His back, straight and precise, seemed to harden. He did not turn.

The collector’s hand closed around Anya’s upper arm.

In that moment, Leo saw it. A flicker in the light of the tank. The two red dots didn’t just pulse. They echoed. A faint, after-image ripple spread from each point, a concentric ring of distortion that washed over the entire map. For a second, the clean geometries of the city grid on the display softened, blurred, as if seen through troubled water.

Vorst saw it too. His head cocked a fraction of an inch.

Anya’s question was not a philosophy. It was a hypothesis. Mikhail, in his raw, screaming connection to whatever the probe had touched, had been a loud signal. Anya was proposing feedback.

Leo’s mind, pared down to its hardest, most useless core, made a connection of its own. The river didn’t care. The mender’s net failed in specific places. You studied the failure to mend the hole.

He looked at his own hands. He looked at Anya, held by the grey, faceless thing. He looked at Mikhail, broken on the floor.

They were the noise.

Make more noise.

He charged the collector holding Anya. Not with his fists. With his whole body, a clumsy, silent projectile. He hit it low, wrapping his arms around its legs. It was like embracing a marble statue. It did not budge. It simply looked down, its lens focusing on the back of his head. Its free hand rose, fingers poised for a precise, disabling touch at the base of his skull.

Anya twisted in its grip. She didn’t try to break free. She reached up with her other hand and grabbed the collector’s smooth, oval face. Not to claw or strike. She pressed her palm flat against its lens, covering its sensor.

The collector froze.

It was a subtle arrest. Its poised hand stopped its descent. Its body locked in perfect equilibrium. For two seconds, three, it was a monument. Then, a low hum emerged from its torso, a pitch that climbed into a whine of strain. On its chest, a small amber light began to strobe.

On the tank, the map dissolved into static.

The clean lines of the future city shattered into a storm of green and red pixels. The two red dots vanished, swallowed in the chaos. A shrill, harmonic alarm blared from the consoles, a sound of pure mathematical distress.

Vorst spun from the tank. His dispassion was gone. His face was tight with a furious, clinical anger. “Release it! You are overloading a local sensor array!”

The second collector released Mikhail and strode toward the struggle. But its movement was wrong. It was stiff, jerky. As it passed the main console bank, the alarm pitch shifted. The lights in the room flickered. The data streams in the wall-conduits stuttered, frozen snapshots of light.

Anya’s interference was a stone in the pond. The ripples were spreading.

Leo, still clinging to the collector’s legs, felt a vibration through the composite. A shudder. He looked up. The collector holding Anya was trembling, a fine, high-frequency vibration. The whine from its core became a screech.

“Let go, Anya!” Leo shouted.

She didn’t. She held on, her jaw clenched, her eyes squeezed shut. She was pouring her presence, her chaotic, dense, human will, directly into its sensor. She was giving it a self to look at, and the self was a storm it could not process.

With a sharp crack, like ice breaking, a fissure appeared in the smooth grey oval of the collector’s face. It spiderwebbed from beneath Anya’s palm. The lens darkened.

The collector’s hand fell from Anya’s arm. It took two stiff, mechanical steps backward, then collapsed to its knees. The hum died. It listed sideways and hit the grate with a heavy, final thud, a felled tree of technology.

The silence was immediate and profound. The alarm cut out. The tank showed only a dull, grey field. The room was lit by the emergency bioluminescence of the consoles, casting long, nervous shadows.

Vorst stared at the fallen collector. His hands, clasped behind his back, were now white-knuckled fists. He looked from it to Anya, who stood panting, rubbing her arm where the grey fingers had been. He looked at Leo, rising from the floor. The dispassionate interest was gone, replaced by something colder: the rage of a perfectionist confronted with a permanent flaw.

“You have not just created noise,” Vorst said, his voice a controlled whisper that carried like a blade. “You have damaged the instrument. You have made the equation unsolvable in this localized frame.”

The second collector stood motionless, its head swivelling between its fallen counterpart and Vorst, as if awaiting corrupted instructions.

Mikhail groaned. He pushed himself up on one elbow. His eyes were clear now, but hollow, scraped out. He looked at the dead machine on the floor. A weak, grim smile touched his lips. “See?” he croaked. “Told you. They don’t like it when you look back.”

Vorst ignored him. He walked to the main console. His fingers danced across the surface, swift and sure. “The audit is concluded. The variables are irredeemably entangled. Standard isolation protocols are insufficient.” He tapped a final sequence. “Initiating local resolution.”

A new sound filled the room. A deep, sub-aural thrum that came up through the grates into the bones of their feet. From the ceiling, hexagonal panels slid back. Armatures descended, each tipped with the same dark, unblinking lenses as the collectors.

The entire chamber was becoming a sensor. A collector itself.

“What is he doing?” Anya whispered.

“He’s not going to study us anymore,” Leo said, the understanding a cold weight in his gut. “He’s going to erase the problem.”

The thrumming intensified. The air grew thick, charged. The hairs on Leo’s arms stood up. He remembered the feeling on the barge, the dense, directionless pull. This was that same gravity, but focused, sharpened to a point. It was not pulling them toward the future. It was pulling them apart.

Mikhail cried out, clutching his head. Anya gasped, doubling over as if punched in the stomach. Leo felt it as a pressure inside his skull, a silent, rising tone that threatened to liquefy his thoughts. It was the drag, refined into a weapon. It didn’t want to take them somewhere. It wanted to unmake them where they stood.

On the floor, the dead collector began to vibrate, its composite shell resonating with the frequency.

Leo fought the pressure, forcing his eyes to focus. Vorst stood at his console, a silhouette against the soft glow, a conductor of this silent, dissolving symphony. The system was perfect. It would remove the error.

The knife was gone. Noise had failed. There was only one source of chaos left.

He lurched toward the tank. The pressure wave made every step feel like wading through solid stone. His vision blurred at the edges. He could hear Anya shouting his name, the sound stretched and thin.

Vorst saw him coming. “It is pointless. The medium is stable. You cannot break it.”

Leo didn’t intend to break it. He reached the great glass tank. The liquid within was perfectly clear, inert. He placed his hands against the cool, smooth surface. He did not strike it. He leaned his forehead against it.

He closed his eyes.

He did not think of the war, or the river, or the flat with its tinny bell. He thought of the net. The mender on the embankment, his small, necessary failures. The holes made by things struggling to get free. He thought of the feeling of the knife in his hand, not as a weapon, but as a thing that had been held, that had cut rope and bread and the bonds of a trapped bird once, a lifetime ago. He thought of Anya’s voice in the dark, a thread. He thought of Mikhail’s laughter on the barge, brittle and real. He gathered the memories, the sensations, the useless, human core of himself. He did not throw them against the glass. He offered them to the liquid. To the perfect receiver.

Listen to this, he thought, pouring the chaotic sum of his life against the barrier. Listen to the noise.

For a moment, nothing.

Then, the liquid in the tank glowed.

It was not the clean red of the signal dots. It was a murky, turbulent gold, the colour of the river at sunset when it carried the silt of a thousand miles. It swirled from where his hands and forehead met the glass, cloudy, imperfect currents forming in the pristine medium.

On the console screens, data-streams erupted into nonsense poetry—strings of numbers bled into fragments of old radio transmissions, weather reports, a child’s singing rhyme. The harmonic thrum in the room faltered, warped. It became a discordant choir of echoes, of memories that were not the system’s own.

Vorst stared, his clinical fury cracking into something like disbelief. “You are… contaminating the medium. With sentiment.”

The pressure lessened. The armatures in the ceiling froze, twitching.

Anya understood first. She stumbled to the tank, placed her hands beside Leo’s. She closed her eyes. Mikhail dragged himself over, leaving a smear on the grate, and pressed his palm flat against the glass below theirs.

The gold cloud bloomed, filling a quarter of the tank. It was a stain. A beautiful, chaotic stain.

The thrumming ceased. The lights normalized. The second collector stood utterly inert, powered down.

Vorst looked from the corrupted tank to his useless controls to the three of them leaning against the glass, breathing heavily. The equation had not been solved. It had been irrevocably soured. The future in this room was no longer clean. It was muddy with the past.

He straightened his grey uniform. The rage was gone, sealed away behind a mask of icy assessment. He was an auditor facing a ledger ruined by an inkblot.

“Local resolution is compromised,” he announced, to the room, to himself. “The experiment is contaminated. The sample is lost.” He looked at them, not as noise anymore, but as a biohazard. “Containment is no longer viable. The only logical recourse is disposal.”

He walked to a section of the bare wall. A panel hissed open at his approach, revealing a small, sleek elevator carriage. He stepped inside.

“The system will purge this sector,” he said, turning to face them as the doors began to close. His face was already receding into shadow. “It will take approximately twelve minutes for the integrity fields to disengage and the river to reclaim this space. You may spend that time contemplating the irony of your victory.”

The doors shut with a soft, final click.

The silence they left behind was different. It was not sterile. It was charged with the hum of damaged machines and the slow, swirling eddies in the tank. The amber light on the dead collector’s chest winked out.

Leo slid down the glass of the tank to sit on the grate. His body felt like a wrung-out rag. Anya slumped beside him. Mikhail lay on his back, staring at the ceiling.

“He’s flooding the place,” Mikhail said, his voice raw.

“Yes,” Leo said.

“So we have twelve minutes to get out of a concrete egg buried in the riverbed.”

“Yes.”

Anya pushed herself to her feet. She walked to the fallen collector, studied it for a second, then began searching the console banks. “He said ‘integrity fields.’ That means this place is holding the water back. There must be a way out. A maintenance lock. An umbilical. Something not on his clean maps.”

Leo forced himself up. The pressure in his head was gone, replaced by a deep, weary ache. He looked at the smooth walls, the sealed door they had entered through. Vorst’s elevator was the only visible exit. He went to the console Vorst had used. The screens were still showing chaotic fragments, but the core interface was intact. He touched the surface. A schematic of the complex appeared. It was a series of concentric circles, like a bullseye buried in the silt. Their chamber was the centre. There were passages, all leading inward. Except one. A thin, service conduit, a spoke in the wheel, leading from the outer ring to a marker labelled Surface Dock A-7.

It was on the opposite side of the complex. They were in the centre. Twelve minutes to navigate a labyrinth built by a man who thought in pure geometry.

“Here,” he said.

Anya and Mikhail crowded around. Mikhail traced the path with a grimy finger. “Long way. Through his machine shop.”

“It’s the only way that doesn’t end in a solid wall,” Anya said. She looked at the door they came in through. “That probably leads back into the maze. Back to the drag.”

“Then we go forward,” Leo said. “Through his heart.”

They moved. Mikhail was weak, leaning on Anya. Leo led, pushing open the heavy door on the far side of the chamber from Vorst’s elevator. It opened onto a corridor that was different from the sterile lab. Here, the walls were raw, reinforced polymer, following the natural curve of the anomaly’s interior. The light was a dim, functional blue. The thrum of machinery was louder, a constant, industrial heartbeat.

They passed open doorways. Rooms filled with racks of grey composite shells—empty collectors, waiting for faces. Rooms with vats of the clear tank medium, bubbling softly. A room that was a forest of crystalline growths, humming with captured energy. It was the workshop of the future, and it was deserted. Vorst had called a purge. His perfect, faceless workers were doubtless elsewhere, securing important things.

The schematic in Leo’s mind was a cold map. Turn left here. Right here. The corridor began to slope upward. The air grew cooler, carrying a new scent underneath the ozone and polymer: damp, and rot, and river.

They reached a junction. A heavy, circular hatch, like a bulkhead on a submarine, was set into the curved wall. According to the map, the service conduit was through it. A wheel lock secured it.

Leo grabbed the wheel. It refused to budge. He threw his weight into it. Nothing. It was sealed from the other side, or locked electronically.

Anya joined him. Together, they strained. The metal groaned but did not turn.

“Time?” Mikhail asked, leaning against the wall, his face ashen.

“Don’t know,” Leo grunted. “Not enough.”

From the corridor behind them, from the direction of the laboratory, a new sound began. A deep, rushing roar, like a distant waterfall. It was growing louder.

The integrity fields were disengging. The river was coming.

Leo braced his foot against the wall and pulled with everything he had. A tendon in his neck stood out. The wheel gave a fraction, then stuck fast.

The roaring grew. They could feel a vibration in the floor now. A fine mist began to seep from the seams of the corridor behind them.

Mikhail pushed himself off the wall. He shuffled to a control panel beside the hatch. It was dark. He looked at it, then at the dead, lensed face of the collector in his memory. He looked at his own trembling hands. He placed them on the dark screen.

He didn’t try to think. He tried to feel. To be the error. The noise in the wire. He thought of the probe touching his temple, the unbearable clarity of the signal, the pain of being a clean point on a map. He pushed that feeling into the dark glass.

The panel flickered. A single, erratic line of green light scrolled across it. With a sharp clunk, the wheel lock disengaged.

Leo spun the wheel. It turned freely. He hauled the heavy hatch open. Beyond was a narrow, pitch-black tunnel, sloping steeply upward. The smell of the river was strong here, cold and alive.

The roar was a thunder now. Around the curve of the corridor behind them, a wall of white, furious water exploded into view, filling the passage, chewing at the machinery, carrying shattered composite and crystalline shards in its maw.

“Go!” Leo yelled.

Anya ducked into the tunnel, pulling Mikhail after her. Leo followed, pulling the hatch shut behind him. He spun the interior wheel just as the water hit the other side. The metal boomed, a colossal, shuddering gong, but it held.

They were in absolute darkness. The sound of the deluge was muffled but immense, a giant pounding on a door. The tunnel was tight, a pipe. They began to climb, hands and feet finding rungs set into the wall. Up and up, into the cold, with the river’s vengeance hammering below them.

Leo lost track of time. There was only the climb, the burn in his muscles, the gasping breaths echoing in the narrow tube. Then, above, a point of grey light. It grew. Fresh, cold air hit his face.

They spilled out onto a metal grating, under a dome of corrugated iron. They were in a small, dilapidated dock, a bubble clinging to the side of the riverbank. Through a grime-encrusted porthole, Leo could see the grey, muscular water of the river flowing past, swollen and angry, carrying debris from the drowned world below.

They had escaped the future.

They lay on the grating, heaving in the cold, real air. The sky through the dock’s open hatch was the colour of old wool, twilight settling on the city.

Mikhail began to laugh, a weak, hiccupping sound. “He purged it. He really did. All of it.”

Anya sat up, hugging her knees. She looked at Leo. “He’s gone. His machine is gone.”

Leo sat with his back against the cold iron wall. He listened to the river, the real river, just beyond the thin shell. It didn’t care about purges or equations. It just moved.

But Vorst was not gone. He had stepped into an elevator. He had looked at them with the eyes of a man who had simply closed a corrupted file. He would open a new one.

The drag was not in the water. It was in the man. And the man was still out there, in the city, his laboratory ruined but his purpose intact. He had audited them. He had found them wanting. He would not stop.

Leo looked at his hands, empty now. The knife was gone, lost in the bright, clean room at the heart of the dark. He had no weapon, no plan. He had only the noise in his head, and the two people breathing beside him in the gloom, and the river’s indifferent song.

The future had a gravity. And they were still falling.



---

Chapter 23

CHAPTER 23

The dock didn’t care that they had escaped. It was a forgotten blister on the river’s flank, a pocket of damp air and rust. The river’s song through the iron skin was a bass note of pure indifference. Leo listened to it until his breathing slowed, until the burn in his arms and legs faded to a dull, familiar ache. He watched a bead of condensation trace a path through the grime on the porthole glass, a slow, meandering descent that had no destination.

Mikhail’s laughter had subsided into silence. He lay on his back on the grating, staring up at the cobwebbed iron dome. Anya had not moved from her hunched position, her chin on her knees, her gaze fixed on nothing.

They had the air of survivors pulled from a wreck, stunned by the sheer fact of the sky. But the wreck was not behind them. It was the city above, waiting. The purge was complete. The machine was gone. The evidence was erased. They were ghosts now, in a world Vorst had finished auditing. They were errors with no file to reference.

Leo pushed himself to his feet. The metal groaned under his weight. He went to the porthole, wiped a circle clear with the heel of his hand. The view was a grey slice of river and the opposite embankment, a wall of soot-blackened stone. No people. No boats. Just the muscular, grey flow, carrying branches, a shattered crate, the dark shape of something that might have been a coat. The future, flushed out to sea.

“He’s not in the river,” Leo said. His voice was raw from the climb, from the bright room. “He’s up there. In the dry air. Thinking.”

“Thinking of what?” Mikhail’s voice was flat. “He won. He cleaned his slate. We’re… we’re just dust on the floor. He’ll sweep us up when he gets to it.”

“No,” Anya said. She didn’t look up. “He doesn’t sweep. He corrects. We’re an inconsistency. He’ll correct us.”

Leo turned from the window. The dock had a single, heavy door set into the curved wall, streaked with rust and verdigris. It looked like it hadn’t been opened in decades. A single, iron lever served as a handle. He walked to it, put his hand on the cold metal. It was the only way out.

“Then we don’t wait for the correction,” he said.

He leaned his weight into the lever. It resisted, seized with corrosion, then gave with a shriek that echoed in the small space. He pulled. The door opened outward, onto a narrow, iron balcony slick with moss. Beyond it, a set of rusted rungs bolted to the stone embankment led up into the gathering dusk.

The cold air was a slap. It was the clean, empty cold of the surface world, not the damp, pressing chill of the under-city. Leo went first, his hands finding the familiar, hateful rhythm of the climb. The rungs were solid, ancient. They climbed past the high-water mark, past stains of old floods, into the twilight.

They emerged behind a tangle of dead brambles at the base of the embankment wall. The city sounds returned, not as a roar, but as a muted, distant sigh. The smell of coal smoke and damp stone. They were in a derelict stretch of the industrial quarter, where the warehouses were hollow-eyed and the cobbles were cracked with weeds. The river was a presence at their backs, a great, grey animal turning in its sleep.

For a long minute, they stood in the shelter of the brambles, watching the empty street. A single, distant streetlamp flickered on, a sickly yellow eye in the gloom.

“Where?” Mikhail whispered. The word was a puff of steam in the cold.

Leo had no answer. The Institute was a flooded tomb. Vorst’s flat would be empty, a shell. The man was a concept now, a pattern of thought moving through the city’s arteries. He would be where the work was. The next file.

Anya shifted beside him. She was looking down at her hands, turning them over as if reading something written on the palms. “The purge was the end of one experiment,” she said, her voice low. “He doesn’t stop. He iterates. He’ll need a new lab. New subjects. New… material.”

“The city is his lab,” Leo said.

“No,” she said, finally looking up. Her eyes were dark pools in the twilight. “The city was his dataset. He’s finished with it. He validated his hypothesis. The noise is removable. The system can be cleaned.” She took a slow breath. “He’ll want to test the next hypothesis. The constructive one. Not removing noise, but… writing the signal.”

The words hung in the cold air. Writing the signal. Not auditing the past, but authoring the future. A colder gravity took hold in Leo’s gut.

“He needs a clean room,” Mikhail said, understanding dawning like a sickness. “Not a flooded basement. A sterile place. A blank page.”

They knew of only one place that fit that description in Vorst’s world. The place he had come from, the place that had funded the Institute, the place that craved order above all else. The Municipal Authority Complex. The great, grey hive of administration that governed the city. It was a place of polished floors and silent corridors, of files that never saw the light, of decisions that flowed downward like law. Vorst had been their contracted visionary. His failure was their embarrassment. His success… his success would be their ultimate tool.

“He’s gone to give his report,” Leo said.

It wasn’t a flight. It was a promotion.

They moved through the dying industrial quarter like stains seeping through old cloth. They kept to the shadows of the warehouses, their footsteps the only sound in the canyons of brick. The normal life of the city—the clatter of trams, the murmur of crowds—felt like a memory, a broadcast from a different frequency. The purge had been silent, invisible, a deletion in a sub-basement, but the city felt lighter for it, emptier, as if a constant, low-grade pain had been switched off. The relief was palpable, and it was a lie.

They found a tram stop on the edge of the commercial district. The few people waiting were hunched against the cold, their faces closed. They boarded a near-empty car, its windows fogged with breath. Leo sat by the window, watching the city slide past. The cafes were half-full. A newsvendor folded his unsold papers. A policeman on a corner stamped his feet. It was a city that believed the war was over, that the last bad thing had happened, and now it was just a matter of mending the net, patient correction after small failure.

He saw the man from his window then, the one with the worn-out coat, mending his net on the embankment. He was a fossil. A remnant of the old, hard core the world had whittled down to. Leo understood him now. The man wasn’t waiting for anything. He was just moving, because movement was the only proof he hadn’t been fully erased. The war hadn’t made him. The peace wouldn’t unmake him. He was just there, a knot of persistent data.

The Municipal Complex rose at the city’s administrative heart, a monolithic slab of pale granite. It had no adornment, no grand stairs. It was a sheer face, punctuated by rows of identical, dark windows. It did not invite; it stated. It was the end of the line for questions.

They got off the tram two blocks away and stood in the doorway of a shuttered pharmacy, looking at it. Lights burned in maybe one in ten windows. The great bronze doors at the base were shut. A single guard in a greatcoat stood in a glass booth to the side.

“We can’t just walk in,” Mikhail said. His face was pale in the reflected streetlight.

“We’re not walking in,” Leo said. “We’re being taken in.”

Anya looked at him. “How?”

“We’re the inconsistency,” Leo said. “We report ourselves.”

He stepped out of the doorway and walked toward the Complex, not to the main doors, but to the guard’s booth. His movements were economical, practiced. He had no plan, only a direction. The future had a gravity, and he was done fighting the fall. He would fall toward the source.

The guard watched him approach with the bored vigilance of a man who sees nothing but drunks and lost clerks. Leo stopped at the glass.

“I need to see whoever is in charge of the Institute contract,” Leo said. His voice was calm, flat. “Tell them Leo is here. And that I have the equation.”

The guard’s eyes, dull with routine, sharpened a fraction. He looked past Leo to where Anya and Mikhail hovered by the pharmacy. He looked back at Leo, at his damp, dirty clothes, his empty hands.

“What equation?”

“The one Vorst used to purge the under-city,” Leo said. “The one he’s about to sell you for the next phase. Tell him I have the counter-equation. Tell him I’ll wait here. He has five minutes.”

It was a bluff built on the architecture of Vorst’s mind. The man dealt in completeness, in elegant solutions. A counter-equation was the one contaminant he could not tolerate. It was noise of the highest order. It would be an itch in his perfect, clean room.

The guard picked up a black telephone receiver, turned his back, spoke in low tones. He hung up, turned around. He didn’t speak. Just watched them through the glass.

The five minutes passed. Then a side door, seamless in the granite wall, clicked open. A man in a dark suit, not a uniform, stood there. He had the face of a headstone—clean, blank, and final. He gestured with a slight tilt of his head.

They went in.

The interior was a shock of silence and light. The floors were polished white marble, the walls a pale, featureless cream. The air was still and temperatureless. Their footsteps echoed like dropped tools in a cathedral. The man in the suit led them without a word to an elevator, its doors a sheet of brushed steel. They entered. He pressed a button for a high floor. The ascent was smooth, silent, profound.

The elevator opened onto a corridor that was a long, pale throat. Doors of dark wood stood shut on either side. At the far end, double doors were slightly ajar, spilling a warmer, yellow light onto the marble. The man in the suit gestured toward them, then remained by the elevator, a sentinel.

They walked the length of the corridor. The silence was a pressure. Leo pushed the double doors open.

It was not an office. It was a viewing room. One wall was a single, vast window looking out over the city, a black tapestry studded with the weak jewels of streetlamps and windows. The other walls were lined with banks of instruments: dials, switches, small screens glowing with green waveforms. In the center of the room, standing at a polished console, was Vorst.

He wore a fresh, dark suit. His hair was damp, combed back. He looked like a man who had just showered after a long, successful day. He did not turn as they entered. He was studying a screen that displayed a simplified, topological map of the city, its under-city a honeycomb of red lines that were now, one by one, blinking out.

“You’re faster than I projected,” Vorst said, his voice conversational. He finally turned. His face was calm, but his eyes held a focused, surgical light. “The river exit. I had it at a forty-three percent probability for your survival. Congratulations.” He said it like a scientist noting an interesting but expected result.

“You left the door open,” Leo said.

“A controlled variable,” Vorst replied. “To see if you’d find it. You did. The data is noted.” He gestured to the room around him. “This is the new laboratory. More appropriate, don’t you think? The substrate here is not stone and water, but policy and permission. A much more malleable medium.”

“You drowned a thousand people,” Mikhail said, the words bursting from him like a cough.

Vorst looked at him as if he had spoken in a slightly crude dialect. “I corrected a corrupted dataset. The under-city was a festering anomaly. A source of continuous, entropic noise. It was making the whole system sick. Now it is clean. The city’s vital signs are already stabilizing.” He tapped a dial on the console with a manicured finger. “Aggregate anxiety levels, trending down. Reported crimes, trending down. Economic throughput… holding steady. The excision was a net positive.”

“They were people,” Anya said, her voice thin with fury.

“They were variables,” Vorst corrected, gently. “Uncontrolled, unmeasured variables. Introducing chaos into the civic equation. My mandate was always systemic health. The health of the organism, Anya, sometimes requires the removal of diseased tissue. You, of all people, should understand that. You who tried to carve the noise out of your own mind.”

Anya flinched as if struck.

Leo took a step further into the room. The window at his back felt like an abyss. “You told us you wanted to hear the music. The signal in the noise.”

“And I did,” Vorst said, a flicker of genuine, chilling passion in his eyes. “I heard it. It was beautiful. And it was drowning. Drowning in the screech of desperation, the static of petty hungers, the feedback loop of fear and superstition. My work was to isolate that signal. To amplify it. But first, I had to eliminate the noise that was masking it. The purge was the final, necessary filter.” He spread his hands, indicating the clean, quiet room. “Now we can begin the real work. With the Authority’s resources, we can design the signal. A harmonious civic frequency. No more conflict. No more waste. Just… efficient, peaceful consensus.”

“You want to control what people think,” Leo said.

“I want to correct what people think,” Vorst said. “To tune them to the proper frequency. To still the internal noise that leads to external disorder. It’s the ultimate public good. A silent, happy, productive city. No more war. No more under-city. No more… you.”

He said it without malice. A simple statement of fact. They were the last remnants of the old noise. The final inconsistency.

“You missed a variable,” Leo said.

Vorst’s head tilted. “Oh?”

“Me.”

Leo took another step. He had nothing in his hands. He had no knife, no weapon. He had only the thing Vorst had never been able to quantify, the thing that had survived the bright room, the purge, the climb. The hard, useless core.

“You audited me,” Leo said, his voice low. “You found my trauma. My noise. You thought it defined me. You thought it was a flaw to be corrected.” He was close to the console now, close enough to see the city map, to see the last red light in the under-city blink out. “But you got it backwards. The noise isn’t the flaw. The silence is. The silence you want, the silence you’re selling… that’s the death. The noise is the life. It’s the friction. It’s the man mending the net, knowing it will tear again. It’s the river that doesn’t care. It’s the thing that keeps moving when the war is over. It’s the only thing worth hearing.”

Vorst smiled, a thin, tolerant curve of the lips. “A poetic defense of suffering. A common romantic fallacy. The signal is beauty, Leo. The noise is pain. I am ending the pain.”

“You’re ending the choice,” Leo said. “You’re ending the fight. That’s all there is. The fight to be a man, not a variable. The fight to hear the river, not your perfect, silent frequency.”

He was not talking to convince Vorst. He was talking to name the thing inside himself. The drag was not in the water. It was in the man. And the man was here, in this sterile room, offering a future of perfect, gravity-less peace. A future with no fall. It was the most terrifying thing Leo had ever seen.

“The conversation is concluded,” Vorst said. He pressed a button on the console. “You are now redundant.”

The doors at the far end of the room opened. Two more men in dark suits entered. They were larger than the first, their movements synchronized, efficient. They did not walk toward Leo; they flanked him, cutting off the path to the door, to Anya and Mikhail.

This was the correction. Neat. Clinical.

Leo looked past them, to the vast window. The city lay below, a pattern of lights Vorst believed he could re-write. He looked at Anya, her face pale but set. He looked at Mikhail, who gave a tiny, almost imperceptible shrug. This is it, the shrug said. This is the fall.

But the future had a gravity. And Leo understood now that you did not fight the fall. You aimed it.

He turned back to Vorst, to the console with its beautiful, deadly dials. He had no weapon. He had only his own persistent, noisy data.

He moved.

Not toward the men. Not toward the door. He moved toward the window. The great, clear sheet of glass that separated the sterile room from the noisy, indifferent, living city below.

The men in suits reacted, but they were programmed for containment, not for this. They moved to intercept him, to grab him. Leo didn’t try to dodge. He let the first one catch his arm, let the second close in. He used their momentum, their solidity, as a pivot. He planted his feet, twisted his body, and threw his weight backward, toward the window, dragging the two men with him in a tangled, stumbling mass.

Vorst’s eyes widened, not with fear, but with pure, computational disbelief. “The window is reinforced—” he began.

But Leo wasn’t trying to break it. Not with his fists.

The three of them—Leo and the two correctors—slammed into the glass. It was thick, it was strong. It shuddered with a deep, resonant groan. The impact was a thunderclap in the quiet room. The console behind Vorst rattled. A glass of water tipped over, spreading a dark stain across a blueprint.

Leo rebounded, fell to the floor. The two men staggered, disoriented.

And in that moment, Mikhail moved. He didn’t go for the men. He went for the console. He had been watching, not the fight, but the machine. He saw the spilled water creeping toward the banks of switches. He saw Vorst, distracted, reaching to right the glass.

Mikhail lunged. His hand, clumsy and desperate, swept across the console, not hitting buttons, but knocking a sheaf of heavy, technical schematics onto the spill. The water soaked into the paper, darkened it, dripped onto the console’s surface.

A spark, small and blue, leapt from a connection. A dial needle swung wildly to zero. One of the green waveform screens fizzed into static.

Vorst hissed, a sound of pure outrage. “Don’t touch that! It’s calibrated!”

It was the first time Leo had heard real emotion in his voice. Not anger, but the fury of a watchmaker seeing a vandal approach his gears.

Anya was moving too. She went not for Vorst, not for the machine, but for the door. She slammed it shut. Then she turned, her back against the dark wood, facing the room. She had no weapon either. She was just a block. A piece of data refusing to be deleted.

Chaos. For three seconds, the sterile room was full of it. The sparking console, the wet blueprint, the two suited men turning from Leo toward Mikhail, Vorst stepping away from the mess with a look of profound disgust, as if his experiment had been soiled by a child.

It was all the noise Leo needed.

He got to his feet. His side ached from the impact. He ignored it. He walked past the confused correctors, past the sputtering machine, straight toward Vorst. Vorst saw him coming. He did not retreat. He stood his ground, his face a mask of cold contempt. He was not a physical man. He was a mind. He believed the physical world would be corrected by men in suits, by systems, by silent waves.

Leo stopped an arm’s length from him. He did not raise his fists.

“You audit everything,” Leo said, his voice quiet, for Vorst alone. “But you never understood the balance sheet. You see life as a liability. Pain as a cost.” He leaned in slightly. “But the cost is the asset. The noise is the signal. You tried to subtract the world into silence. But I’m adding myself back in.”

He reached out then, not to strike Vorst, but to grab the lapel of his perfect, dark suit. It was a crude gesture. A messy, human, noisy gesture.

Vorst looked down at Leo’s hand on his coat, his disgust absolute. “This proves nothing. This is just… friction.”

“Yes,” Leo said.

And he pulled Vorst forward, off-balance, and threw his weight backward again, not toward the window this time, but toward the sparkling, shorting console.

They fell together. Vorst, the mind, and Leo, the noise. They hit the edge of the console. More papers scattered. A bank of switches snapped. A screen went dark. Vorst cried out—a short, sharp sound of pure intellectual pain—as his temple struck the edge of the metal. He slumped to the floor, a dark trickle of blood immediately welling in his silver hair.

The two correctors froze, staring at their fallen principal. The logic of their world had just short-circuited.

The room was silent again, but a different silence. A broken silence. Full of the smell of ozone, of wet paper, of blood.

Leo stood over Vorst, breathing hard. The man’s eyes were open, blinking up at the ceiling, confused. The perfect calculation was gone. There was only pain, and surprise.

Mikhail was staring at the ruined console, his hands held away from his body as if they were contaminated. Anya still leaned against the door, her chest rising and falling rapidly.

Leo looked down at Vorst. “The correction,” he said, “is incomplete.”

He turned. He walked to the great window. He placed his palm against the cold, strong glass. Below, the city’s lights glittered, a chaotic, beautiful, noisy constellation. The river was a black vein through its heart, moving, indifferent, carrying all things away in time.

They had stopped the next file from being opened. They had introduced a fatal error into the clean room. But the man was still breathing on the floor. The Authority was still in the building. The gravity was still there.

He turned back to the room. He looked at Anya, at Mikhail. They were with him. They were the noise. They were the counter-equation.

“We have to go,” he said. “Now.”

They stepped over Vorst, past the stunned correctors, and opened the door. The pale throat of the corridor awaited. The elevator was at the far end, the sentinel still beside it. He would have heard the noise. He would be waiting.

They had escaped the future again. But the future was not a place. It was a force. And it was waiting for them in the silent, marble hall.
